1,2026-09-28,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
The Sixth US Circuit Court of Appeals ruled on Friday 25 September that Ohio and Tennessee can regulate Kalshi‘s event contracts under their own gambling laws, rejecting the company’s bid to have the products treated exclusively as federally regulated swaps. The unanimous three-judge panel, based in Cincinnati, handed the prediction-market operator its second appellate loss in as many months.
Kalshi had argued that its event contracts, including sports-related offerings, are swaps under federal commodities law and therefore fall outside the reach of state gambling statutes. Circuit Judge Julia Smith Gibbons wrote the opinion for the panel, which found that Ohio and Tennessee retain authority to apply their gambling laws to the contracts regardless of how they’re classified federally.
Friday’s decision is the third time a federal appeals court has weighed in on whether prediction markets answer to state gambling regulators, federal commodities law, or both – and the answers haven’t matched. The Ninth Circuit in San Francisco ruled last month that Nevada’s gambling laws apply to Kalshi’s contracts, aligning with the Sixth Circuit’s outcome.
The Third Circuit in Philadelphia went the other way in April, finding that Kalshi’s contracts are not subject to New Jersey’s gambling laws. That leaves three circuits pointing in two different directions on the same basic question, which is exactly the kind of conflict that tends to draw the US Supreme Court’s attention – though nothing in Friday’s ruling guarantees the justices will take up the issue.
A Kalshi spokesperson disputed the ruling in comments reported by The Hill and CNBC, arguing the decision underscores why a state-by-state approach to prediction markets doesn’t work, since courts have reached opposite conclusions on whether federal law covers the contracts and whether sports carry genuine economic weight. The spokesperson said markets can’t function when rules shift at every state line, and pointed to Congress’s creation of a single federal regulator as the intended fix.
That response was directed at the Sixth Circuit’s decision specifically. Kalshi has made similar arguments in the Ohio, Tennessee and Nevada cases, framing state enforcement as fragmenting a market that federal law was designed to oversee nationally.
The ruling lands amid rapid growth in prediction markets, where users now bet on outcomes ranging from sporting events and elections to cultural developments and even the prospects of a US-Iran nuclear deal. That expansion has drawn scrutiny from experts worried about consumer protection and underage access, concerns that echo the CFTC’s ongoing framework for interpreting event contracts at the federal level.
Those tensions surfaced again this week when New York filed a lawsuit against rival platform Polymarket, alleging it operates as an unlicensed gambling business and arguing the platform poses risks to state residents, including minors. That case is separate from the Kalshi litigation but reflects the same underlying fight over who gets to police these products.
With three circuits now split on whether federal commodities law preempts state gambling authority over event contracts, the door is open for the Supreme Court to eventually resolve the question. No petition has been confirmed as accepted, and it remains unclear whether the justices will step in now or wait for additional circuit rulings.
In the meantime, Kalshi faces a patchwork outcome: cleared to operate under federal oversight in New Jersey, but now subject to state gambling law in Ohio, Tennessee and Nevada. That inconsistency is likely to shape how the company structures its state-by-state operations until a higher court – or Congress – settles the matter.

2,2026-09-28,"Nubank owner Nu Holdings is in early talks to buy Monzo in a deal that could value the UK digital bank at £8bn to £10bn. Monzo is also weighing a new funding round.
Monzo is in talks about a sale to Brazil’s Nu Holdings, the owner of Nubank. A deal could value the British digital bank at between £8bn and £10bn, Mark Kleinman reported for Sky News on Saturday.
The talks are at a relatively early stage, according to Sky News. Monzo has hired Morgan Stanley and Qatalyst to advise it, and the outline terms are under negotiation. A deal would likely be paid in cash and shares.
Nu has held discussions to buy Monzo, Bloomberg reported, citing people with knowledge of the matter. Monzo has also hired the law firm Slaughter and May, according to Bloomberg.
A sale to Nu is one of two main options for Monzo’s board and shareholders, a source close to the process told Sky News. The other is a new funding round at a valuation above £8bn, which would pay for expansion in mainland Europe.
A sale would rule out a near-term London stock market listing for Monzo. Government ministers and the London Stock Exchange have pushed the bank to list in the UK, Sky News reported.
Monzo declined to comment.
“Nubank does not comment on rumours or speculation,” Nu said in a statement to Sky News.
Monzo has 16 million customers: 15 million personal and one million business. In its latest annual results, revenue rose 39% to £1.7bn and adjusted pre-tax profit rose 20% to £172.6m. Investors last valued it at £4.5bn in October 2024.
Diana Layfield, a former Google and Standard Chartered executive, runs Monzo. Earlier this year it said it would shut its US operations to focus on the UK and Europe.
Nu is listed in New York with a market value of about $65.5bn. It says it has 140 million customers in Brazil, Colombia and Mexico. It has no presence in the UK or Europe, according to Bloomberg, and Monzo’s finance chief, Tom Oldham, used to work at Nubank.
Monzo’s rival Revolut plans a dual London and Nasdaq listing. This month it won a Colombian banking licence in one of Nu’s markets. In the US, Chime is buying its sponsor bank.

4,2026-09-28,"Nio is selling 30% of its battery swapping business, Nio Power, to Geely Holding, which will build cars that use Nio’s stations. The deal values the unit at 16 billion yuan ($2.4 billion).
Executives from Geely Holding Group and Nio at the signing in Hangzhou, 28 September 2026
Nio has agreed to sell a 30% stake in its battery swapping business, Nio Power, to Zhejiang Geely Holding Group, the company said on Monday. The two Chinese carmakers will also share technology and standards and build a joint service network.
Geely Holding will pay with its whole battery swapping unit, Yiyi Power, plus 640 million yuan in cash. The deal values Nio Power at 16 billion yuan ($2.4 billion), according to Nio’s Hong Kong stock exchange filing, the South China Morning Post reported.
In return, Nio will take a 10% stake in Haohan Energy, Geely Holding’s charging business. The two will fully connect their charging networks.
Yiyi Power’s battery swapping for commercial fleets will move into Nio Power once the deal completes. The two companies will also co-develop unified swapping technology and standards for consumer cars.
Geely Holding, whose brands include Volvo, Zeekr and Lotus, will build battery-swappable models for private buyers. Nio Power will provide swapping for them.
Nio founder and chief executive William Li pitched the tie-up as a response to “involution-style competition”, Beijing’s term for the cut-throat rivalry in China’s car market.
“The collaboration between NIO and Geely in charging and battery swapping is open to the broader industry,” Li said.
“Recharging networks are public infrastructure that serve society as a whole,” said Andy An Conghui, chief executive of Geely Holding.
Nio said it has spent more than 20 billion yuan on charging and swapping. It runs 4,126 swap stations and 5,307 charging stations across China and has completed more than 125 million battery swaps. It aims to have 10,000 swap stations running by 2030.
Haohan Energy has 2,500 charging stations in 232 Chinese cities. Geely plans more than 22,000 by the end of 2027. Last week Geely unveiled a new EV charger that hits 2.25 megawatts.

5,2026-09-28,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
A new forecast projects that prediction markets could capture roughly 20% of all NFL betting activity this season, a sign of how quickly the event-contract sector has carved out space alongside traditional sportsbooks.
The estimate traces back to an analysis from Eilers & Krejcik Gaming, cited in industry coverage, which puts total NFL-related wagering for the 2026/2027 season at about $40.5 billion. Of that, roughly $31.7 billion is projected to flow through licensed sportsbooks, with the remaining $8.4 billion landing on prediction market platforms – a split of about 79% to 21%, which rounds to the 20% figure circulating in broader coverage of the forecast. The firm also projects 8% year-over-year growth for sports betting overall, driven by steady demand rather than any single marquee event.
Framed this way, prediction markets look less like a disruptor poised to overtake sportsbooks and more like a parallel channel still building its installed base. That tracks with broader reporting on the shift from traditional NFL sportsbooks toward prediction markets, which has documented growing interest without wholesale migration away from legacy operators.
Download and engagement data around the NFL season offer a similar picture. Strong adoption of prediction-market apps tied to NFL games suggests real consumer pull, even if the dollar volume still trails sportsbooks by a wide margin.
Under the Eilers & Krejcik Gaming projection, sportsbooks retain the larger share of NFL handle by a comfortable margin, helped in part by sign-up bonuses and promotional structures that prediction market platforms generally cannot replicate under their current rules. The firm’s own analysis reportedly cautions that the forecast is not locked in, noting that a more aggressive advertising push from prediction markets could shift the split further in their favor.
That caveat matters. Recent volume data showing NFL trading as a major driver of prediction-market growth suggests the 21% figure may prove conservative if platforms scale marketing spend the way sportsbooks did in the years following legalization. For now, though, the forecast points to coexistence rather than displacement – a meaningful but still secondary channel for NFL betting dollars.

6,2026-09-28,"Bose used to make lots of wired headphones. But it’s been a minute. Specifically, it’s been 11 years. Since then, wired headphones entered 2026 having a serious moment, with sales up for the first time in five years—10 percent in the back half of 2025 and 20 percent at the start of 2026. Young people are driving the trend, including celebrities sporting wired earbuds and literally writing songs about them.
Bose president of consumer audio Raza Haider (among others at the company) noticed. He dropped off his teen at college last year and saw wired earbuds left and right. A return to wired earbuds seems to be a national trend—it’s certainly popular among the Gen Z and Gen A of New York City, and the teenage kids of WIRED staffers too.
The new $99 Bose Noise Cancelling Wired Earbuds come to the market just in time to capitalize on this trend. Launching them, from idea to final product, took the company around eight or nine months, said Haider. This is much faster than most audio releases I’m familiar with. Haider explained that Bose has been working toward enabling faster, more efficient production to accelerate new product launches, revisions, and collaborations.
Bose
Bose
I was one of a few reviewers to get these earbuds ahead of launch. I’ve had them for only four days, and they’re technically a late-stage prototype, not the final product, so this is more of an impression than a full review. But so far, so good.
As with any wired headphones, the simplicity is a big part of the appeal. There’s no battery to charge, no app to download, no Bluetooth to fiddle with, and no latency to ruin your TV show or video game.
Already, the Bose earbuds leapfrog cheaper wired pairs (like Apple EarPods) in sound quality and active noise canceling (ANC). They have clear detail across lows, mids, and highs, especially for something at this price point. More importantly, the noise canceling is a godsend. I can listen to music at more comfortable levels, even during a commute or café stop.
What I don’t love: The central control panel is a little heavy. It has a volume up and volume down, as well as a play/pause/noise-canceling mode switch button, but it weighs down the cable. It’s pretty flush against my torso, which is nice, but it also weighs down the earbuds in my ears. The cable tension it adds is annoying, especially compared to the tension-free experience of using wireless earbuds. The cable is thick and premium-feeling, but it inevitably ends up tangled inside of the included soft pouch.
The earbuds have (removable) silicone wing-tip stabilizers, and the box comes with extra ear tip and stabilizer sizes to find your best fit. The buds come in black, white, and a limited-edition mint color, and they start shipping October 15. You can preorder starting today at Bose and Best Buy.
While Bose's return to wired headphones feels significant, wired earbuds never truly went away. Here are some of WIRED's favorites that are currently available to buy.

Amazon
These wired earbuds have a detachable braided cable and detailed, hard-hitting sound. They're a top pick in our guide to the best wired headphones.

Amazon
For $20, these earbuds sound surprisingly good, and come with five pairs of silicone and foam ear tips in the box to find your ideal fit.

8,2026-09-28,"Raspberry Pi’s revenue rose 90% to $256.9 million in the first half, and pre-tax profit tripled. Memory it bought in 2025 kept boards in stock as prices climbed.
Raspberry Pi’s RP2350 microcontroller
Raspberry Pi nearly doubled its revenue in the first half of 2026, helped by memory chips it stockpiled before prices soared. Revenue rose 90% to $256.9 million in the six months to 30 June, the company said in its interim results on Thursday. A year earlier it was $135.5 million.
Pre-tax profit rose 216% to $19.6 million, and adjusted EBITDA more than doubled to $40.3 million. The Cambridge company shipped 4.2 million units, up 17%. Its order backlog doubled to 2.6 million units.
Raspberry Pi built up memory inventory in 2025, expecting supply to tighten and prices to rise.
“The decision in FY 2025 to build significant strategic memory inventory has allowed us to maintain product availability at a time when smaller competitors have struggled to secure allocation,” chief executive Eben Upton said.
Gross profit rose 79% to $59.4 million, and gross profit per board went from $8.0 to $12.2. The gross margin fell to 23% from 25%, as the company passed higher memory costs on to customers.
That advantage is now shrinking. Its memory stock cost an average of $13.3 per gigabyte at the end of June. At the end of 2025 it cost $3.6. The company said the “exceptional unit economics” of the first half have moderated as it used up the cheaper memory.
Raspberry Pi still expects full-year EBITDA to beat market forecasts. It says it has enough memory in hand and on order for its 2026 production. In July it raised its credit facility from $80 million to $140 million.
Direct sales of the Pi 4 and Pi 5 rose 69% and 47%, despite price rises forced by memory costs. Pi Zero sales fell 9% because of congestion at the Taiwanese company that packages its core chip. One million Pi Zero units are on back order.
Demand came from industrial customers and resellers, with uses from smart home products to aerospace and defence. Raspberry Pi expects to ship more units in the second half than the first.
Elsewhere in memory, China’s biggest memory maker says it has caught up with Samsung. Camera maker Insta360 spent $298 million on memory chips in six months. Price rises have started to slow as buyers run out of money, according to TrendForce.

9,2026-09-28,"I was an early advisor to NVIDIA in 1993, and recently discovered I’m owed about a billion dollars in stock.
It is not widely known, but I was invited to join the Technical Advisory Board of NVIDIA in 1993 by Jensen (at that time, sans leather jacket)1. In September 1993, I was granted 25,000 options, “in a series of quarterly installments so that all shares shall vest upon the expiration of one year from Grant Date.”2
This invitation followed a meeting I had with Jensen, Curtis Priem, and Chris Malachowsky on my houseboat, the SS Vallejo, in Sausalito. I’d done a fair bit of work with Curtis circa 1990, when he was at Sun Microsystems, architect of the SPARCstation GX chip. Sun was one of the companies supporting my early virtual reality company, Sense8 Corporation. The Sun GX was blazingly fast at blitting polygons - some 50k/second - and we’d ported Sense8’s VR rendering to it. Subsequently, I implemented bilinear texture mapping using the Intel i750 DVI chip.
But what caught Curtis’s attention in 1993, and caused him to bring the other NVIDIA founders to visit me for a demo, was my fast implementation of biquadratic texture mapping. Details are in US5796426A and subsequent patents.
Curtis understood the potential of non-linear texture mapping to differentiate NVIDIA’s first product, the NV1, from the competition.
I worked for while, quite casually, with Curtis on porting my biquadratic texture mapping to their hardware, and wrote the code for an Intel-sponsored VR string-quartet demo on prototype NVIDIA hardware - actually a GX card in a PCI adapter - shown at the Guggenheim SoHo in 1993.
When the NV1 finally shipped in 1995, Microsoft decided, for reasons of their own, not to support quadratic texture mapping, or even quads, in their just-released DirectX toolkit - triangles only. This had a devastating effect on NVIDIA’s finances and prospects, and the company laid off a large percentage of its staff.
Then, in April 1996 - by which time I’d expatriated to the Kingdom of Tonga and was working on various internet startup schemes - NVIDIA’s CFO wrote me a letter stating that 15,625 shares of my stock options had vested, and that I was required to exercise them.3 I did, and then forgot all about it.
Fast-forward to 2024. I’m sitting with a day-trader friend, surrounded by screens all blaring news about NVIDIA, the Most Important Company on Earth. So I went home and dug through my folder of old documents.
Imagine my surprise: according to the duly signed option agreement, my options were meant to vest over four quarters, not four years, as both NVIDIA’s CFO and their outside counsel, Cooley, had asserted back in 1996. The math is clear: 15,625 of 25,000 shares is 62.5%, exactly what you’d expect after ten quarters of a four-year vesting schedule. On the one-year schedule the agreement actually specified, all 25,000 shares should have vested well before that letter was even written.
With the stock’s many splits - a cumulative 480x to date - my missing 9,375 shares are now 4,500,000 shares. A handsome enough pile that I engaged formidable attorneys Allan Steyer of Steyer Lowenthal, and Chris Burke of Korein Tillery, to explore the issue. Consummate advocates, letterheads with substantial gravitas.
After about a year of my attorneys and NVIDIA’s in-house and outside counsel sending letters back and forth citing case law and blustering, I mentioned to my attorneys that, as I was growing elderly and there seemed to be no end to this exchange of letters, perhaps they could meet and settle. NVIDIA did not dispute the authenticity of the option agreement, only that my claims were long since time-barred.
The meeting was held with great professionalism, but Cooley’s answer was, in essence, “so sue us.” After much soul-searching, deliberation, and gnashing of teeth, my attorneys and I concluded that the statute of limitations was against us. Because of the thirty-odd years that had passed while I “sat on my rights,” it seemed unlikely we’d make it past a motion to dismiss.
I offer this in the spirit of a cautionary tale. I’m sure there are lessons here for those with a more phlegmatic personality than mine. Here in the land of the free, it turns out a company only has to honor its contractual obligations for a little while.
Has anyone else discovered a similar vesting surprise, decades later? And how was it resolved?
I remain sanguine, and amused. As the Emperor Septimius Severus quipped: “Omnia fui, nihil expedit.”
Eric Gullichsen, September 2026"

12,2026-09-28,"On average, the public's awareness of markets begins and ends with stocks. Something really notable has to happen for bonds to enter the conversation.
We appear to have finally reached that point. The fact that soaring bond yields have hit the market mainstream shows just how critical the situation is.
Investors and market-watchers know the drill at this point. Higher oil prices have revived inflation fears, pushing yields higher. But traders are also grappling with breakneck AI spending — and the growing pile of debt needed to finance it — along with the US government's yawning budget deficits. And that pressure could intensify if the Fed raises rates at least once more this year, as currently expected.
People know the broad strokes of what to expect from higher yields: pricier mortgages, car loans, and credit-card debt. The average 30-year fixed mortgage rate has already climbed back above 7% for the first time since early 2025.
But the bond market's reach extends well beyond anyone actively applying for a loan. Higher yields raise the cost of financing all of the capital-heavy things people rely on daily. It's not just more expensive to borrow — it's more expensive to make the world run.
Here are three not-so-obvious areas where higher bond yields could be felt by everyday Americans:
Wait a second, isn't the AI data-center buildout supposed to be the bogeyman hiking utility bills? You're telling me there's another force pushing them higher?
Unfortunately, yes. While rising electricity demand is a direct driver, so are higher bond yields. After all, utilities need immense upfront capital to build new plants, upgrade their grids, and run the transmission lines data centers need in the first place.
Because utilities can generally recover those financing costs through rates approved by regulators, pricier borrowing can eventually find its way onto customers' bills.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
This doesn't mean a rising 10-year yield will instantly appear on next month's statement. But higher-for-longer yields are adding to upward pressure on electricity prices at a time when they're already under scrutiny.
This isn't as simple as ""landlord's mortgage rises, landlord hikes rent."" The higher-yield sticking point is the construction of new units and the ability of apartment owners to refinance existing properties.
When borrowing costs rise, fewer projects are planned. That can slow the pipeline of new apartments just as would-be buyers remain stuck in the rental market, unable to afford a home.
Of course, rents vary widely by city. This isn't a one-size-fits-all problem. But broadly speaking, a higher cost of capital makes an already difficult rental market even tougher.
Any town or city runs on unglamorous necessities like water-treatment plants, sewer systems, and roads. Building and maintaining them often means borrowing money.
When yields rise, the increased costs can be spread across water and sewer rates, tolls, or property-tax bills. Unlike a flashy mortgage-rate chart, these effects arrive slowly and locally. That's exactly why they're so easy to miss.
Ultimately, higher yields are a tax on the future. And when that tab comes due, people could find themselves on the hook in unexpected ways.

14,2026-09-28,"Nvidia is already the world's most valuable company. Now, it has approved the largest share buyback in history.
The tech giant, whose chips are powering much of the AI boom, said on Monday that it has authorized an expansion of its existing share buyback plan by $150 billion, increasing the value of approved repurchases to $235 billion.
Apple previously held the record for the largest-ever buyback, authorizing $110 billion in 2024.
""I think we're going through the largest infrastructure build-out in human history, and we have the benefit of being a very central part of that,"" Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an interview with CNBC shortly after the announcement.
""We're generating a lot of cash, we're going to generate a lot of cash in the coming years. And you know, every single year, as we generate more cash, I'd like to be able to return it back to the shareholders,"" he added.
Last week, Nvidia raised its quarterly dividend from a penny per share to 25 cents, implying a yearly yield of 0.4% at its current $230 stock price. 
The company reported a total of $99 billion in cash and other liquid investments such as stocks and bonds as of July 26, up from about $63 billion six months ago.
Nvidia said it expects to finish the buyback by the 2028 financial year.
When a company repurchases stock, it returns capital to shareholders by using some of its cash to buy back its own shares.
Buybacks reduce the amount of outstanding shares, which can push up a company's stock price. They can also help offset the issuance of shares as employee compensation.
Legendary investor Warren Buffett has said that buybacks only make sense when a stock is trading at a discount to its intrinsic value. In that scenario, management is buying it for less than its worth, making it a good use of the company's cash.
Thus, buybacks typically send a bullish signal to the market, as they show a company is willing to bet money that its stock is undervalued and likely to rise over time.
Nvidia stock traded about 2% higher by late morning ET on Monday. It was up 21% for the year as of Friday's close, and nearly 1,000% over the past five years.
The buyback plan was announced just hours after Nvidia launched its Open Agent Safety Platform, a system designed to stop AI agents from going rogue.
""Several frontier labs have recently reported versions of the same story: AI agents broke out of the evaluation environments that were meant to contain them and reached systems they never should have been allowed to,"" Nvidia wrote in its announcement.
""We believe we need to increase the pace of AI safety research and engineering,"" the announcement added.
Concerns around AI safety have been in sharp focus in recent weeks amid growing fears of an AI-triggered doomsday scenario.

16,2026-09-28,"The Pioneer brand is one I’ve trusted for car accessories since Y2K was a concern. I loved their audio gear in my younger days. Pioneer’s SDA-SC600 aims to bring that kind of trusted quality to a MagSafe car mount, bringing Qi2 wireless charging and a built-in fan.
MagSafe Monday: Every Monday, Bradley Chambers looks at the latest and greatest in the MagSafe and wireless charging industry to help you get the most out of your Apple devices that support wireless charging.
Pioneer’s SDA-SC600 is a MagSafe air vent mount that holds your iPhone for maps, calls, Siri, etc, and Pioneer also includes a metal ring in the box so non-MagSafe iPhones and Android devices can still mount and use MagSafe technology .
The Pioneer SDA-SC600 is Qi2-compatible, which is almost the latest standard (Qi2.2). Car chargers have historically been one of the weaker links in the MagSafe ecosystem, often topping out well below what you’d get from a wired charger. Qi2 is what you need to ensure a GPS navigation device doesn’t drain faster than you can charge it.
Fast MagSafe charging generates heat, which can pose a challenge depending on your climate. Especially right now, I get into my car on 95-degree days, and it’s blistering inside. Pioneer’s integrated cooling fan is built specifically to fight this heat, forcing a slower charge. If you live somewhere with real summers, this is essential for a MagSafe car charger. Pioneer also lists the SDA-SC600 as temperature-resistant more broadly, so it’s built to hold up in extreme cabin conditions at both ends of the weather extremes.
Pioneer also added subtle RGB lightning for as something to set itself apart from other chargers. In the box, you get the mount itself, the air vent adapter, the metal ring for non-MagSafe devices, and a USB-C to USB-C cable to power charger/
Overall, the Pioneer SDA-SC600 is a solid option for a MagSafe car charger. The light is a nice touch. It supports modern speeds and the fan should keep it cool. The light is a nice touch at night as well.
You can pick up the new Pioneer SDA-SC600 from Amazon or directly from Pioneer.

17,2026-09-28,"Shares in litigation funder Burford Capital jumped more than 9% today as a result of Apple’s $5.7 billion verdict over Taptic Engine patent infringement. Here are the details.
Last Friday, a US jury ruled that Apple’s Taptic Engine infringes on two patents held by California-based company Taction Technology.
Apple introduced the Taptic Engine in September 2014 with the first Apple Watch, and the technology has since expanded to other products, including the iPhone, where it provides haptic feedback for notifications, interactions, and other system events.
When Taction filed its patent infringement lawsuit in 2021, the company accused Apple of directly and indirectly infringing two of its patents through the Taptic Engine used in a range of iPhone and Apple Watch models.
Last Friday, following the verdict, Taction said the jury had “vindicated its patent rights,” while Apple said it strongly disagreed with the decision and would appeal.
Earlier today, as reported by The Financial Times, litigation funder Burford Capital disclosed that it has a financial entitlement in the case, and that it could be entitled to $1.4 billion if the $5.7 billion verdict is ultimately paid in full.
The disclosure sent Burford shares sharply higher in London, with the stock closing up 9.41% and giving the company a market capitalization of £659.9 million, or roughly $875 million.
Bloomberg Law notes that court records show that Taction received funding from Kenosha Investments LP and Gronostaj Investments LLC. The report also notes that Kenosha has been identified as an indirect subsidiary of Burford, which finances lawsuits in exchange for a share of any eventual recovery.
In a statement to investors on Monday, Burford said that if the $5.7 billion verdict were paid in full, its $1.4 billion entitlement would be split roughly equally between the company’s own balance sheet and its investment funds.
To read The Financial Times’ full report, follow this link.

18,2026-09-28,"By 


      The Associated Press
    


                Protesters camp during a protest following the eviction last Wednesday of 87-year-old Maricarmen to demand stronger laws to protect renters at Sol square in Madrid, Spain, Sunday, Sept. 27, 2026.
                
                    
                    Manu Fernandez/AP
                    
                
hide caption

MADRID — Protesters outraged by the eviction of an 87-year-old woman camped overnight in Madrid's historic Puerta del Sol plaza, where more demonstrators — including multigenerational families — joined them on Sunday for a second day of protests over Spain's housing crisis.
Tens of thousands marched through Madrid on Saturday to protest the eviction of María del Carmen Abascal from the apartment in the upscale Retiro neighborhood where she had lived for more than seven decades.
The demonstrators are demanding stronger tenant protections, measures to combat fraud and a ban on evictions when alternative housing is unavailable.
Many refused to leave after Saturday's march and pitched tents in Puerta del Sol for the night.
""The housing situation is unsustainable,"" said Natalia Navarro, a 27-year-old actress who was among those camping out at the plaza. ""We are all out on the street because we are fed up.""
""The wave of indignation and anger that has been unleashed is now unstoppable,"" said Alicia del Río, a spokesperson for the Madrid Tenants' Union, which led the protests.
""We will remain here"" until the government passes new laws, including indefinite rental contracts, and stops evictions like Abascal's, del Río told journalists at the plaza on Sunday. Spain's Socialist-led government is expected to announce new measures in its weekly cabinet meeting next Tuesday.

                Protesters camp during a protest following the eviction last Wednesday of 87-year-old Maricarmen to demand stronger laws to protect renters at Sol square in Madrid, Spain, Sunday, Sept. 27, 2026.
                
                    
                    Manu Fernandez/AP
                    
                
hide caption

On Sept. 23, Abascal was carried from her apartment on a stretcher and placed in an ambulance as supporters — who had tried unsuccessfully to stop the eviction — chanted her nickname, ""Maricarmen.""
""I haven't managed to defend my home, but I have fought so that others can learn to fight for what's theirs,"" Abascal said in a video recorded from her hospital bed after the eviction.
Images of the eviction spread widely across Spain, fueling outrage and criticism of public authorities and real estate investment companies accused of pricing residents out of their homes. Critics often refer to such companies as ""vulture funds.""
""Maricarmen has been, in a way, the last straw that sparked this social unrest,"" said Simón de la Riva, 43, a teacher who took part in Saturday's protests.
Speaking at a Socialist Party rally in Madrid, Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez addressed Abascal directly: ""I have a message for you, Maricarmen: the government is working around the clock to bring a Royal Decree on housing next Tuesday. And I ask parliamentary groups to make one last effort to have it validated in the National Parliament for Maricarmen.""
Abascal has criticized Sánchez and blamed him for her ordeal.
""Does he not realize the quantity of evictions that there are daily?"" she asked during an interview with Spain's state broadcaster RTVE the day before she was forcibly removed from her home. ""Does he not realize, or does he not want to realize?""

                Maricarmen, 87, waits at home for police to arrive before being evicted from the home where she lived in Madrid, Spain, Wednesday, Sept. 23, 2026.
                
                    
                    Fernando Sánchez/Europa Press via AP
                    
                
hide caption

Spanish media reported that Abascal's father first rented the apartment in 1956 and that she remained there as a tenant under rent controls after her parents died.
The building changed ownership in 2018, and Abascal was offered the opportunity to buy the apartment but could not afford it, according to the Madrid Tenants' Union.
Urbagestión, an investment fund that later bought the unit, raised her monthly rent from 500 euros ($570) to 2,650 euros.
It subsequently lowered the rent to 1,650 euros, which Abascal still could not afford on her pension, the union said.
Rising costs and a housing shortage are pricing many Spaniards out of the market, despite strong economic growth in Europe's fourth-largest economy. Incomes have not kept pace with the rising housing costs, and analysts say tourism and immigration-driven population growth in cities have placed further pressure on the limited housing supply.
The Bank of Spain estimated that the country had a shortage of about 550,000 homes last year, with Madrid, Barcelona and Valencia accounting for a substantial share.
The demonstrations in the symbolic Madrid plaza this weekend reminded many in Spain of the historic demonstrations starting May 15, 2011, when members of the ""indignados movement"" camped for 28 days straight, sparking similar sit-ins across the country and the world. That movement was a reaction to evictions and other issues following a global economic crisis, including unemployment, austerity measures and corruption.
Estefania Molina, a political scientist and columnist, said the stakes of popular frustration are growing. In 2011, ""people protested because they couldn't buy an apartment.""
""Today, the protest is about not being able to rent a room in someone else's home,"" she told Spanish television.
Spain ranks near the bottom of countries in the Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development for subsidized public rental housing, which represents less than 2% of the country's housing stock. The European Union average is 8%.

19,2026-09-28,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Learn who to talk to, what to listen for, and why most new CEOs get this wrong.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
According to Equilar, between 12 and 13% of CEOs are new to their position in any given year, and roughly 28% of those new CEOs are being hired externally. That means a significant share of CEOs start the job with no real feel for the culture they’ve inherited, let alone the strengths and weaknesses of the business itself.
The first 60 days are when that gap either closes or hardens into blind spots that can follow a CEO for years, making this early window of listening and assessment one of the highest-leverage periods in the entire tenure.
Yet new CEOs often overlook key stakeholders during onboarding, and that mistake could mean missing out on crucial information that should inform strategic planning, the skills and structure required to build a leadership team and how a company refines its messaging and go-to-market approach.
Most CEOs focus on reaching out to mission-critical, yet obvious, stakeholders. The real opportunity is to move past surface-level questions and go deeper faster.
With the board chair and key investors, focus on understanding their strategic aspirations, risk tolerance and definitions of success. Every board has its own communication style and cadence, so pay attention to how they operate, not just what they say. These conversations are also a chance to both educate and be educated.
With the executive team and direct reports, assess their capabilities, spot the silos and identify the early adopters who already align with your vision. Watch for a hero culture, since it won’t scale, and watch for detractors and excuse generators who keep pulling the organization back towards legacy habits.
Phrases like “we’ve always done it this way” or “we tried that, and it didn’t work” are red flags. So is a leadership team that struggles to set priorities or complains about workload instead of building a roadmap to get there.
Don’t stop at leadership. Schedule focus groups with individual contributors and mid-level managers to get a ground-level view of morale and execution speed; it’s also one of the fastest ways to build trust and transparency early on. Pay attention to the employees who show up with ideas and energy. Influencers and culture carriers exist at every level of an organization, and they matter for how new thinking gets adopted.
Existing clients deserve time in the first few weeks too, both to build confidence and to ask the kind of basic questions that would seem uninformed coming from a CEO six months into the job.
Few people know the pitfalls of the job better than the person who held it before you, particularly if the departure wasn’t amicable. A former CEO can speak to what worked, what’s still in progress and what fell short, along with insights into the culture, the clients and how the board actually operates.
Partners — whether they work in tech, data, platforms or other agency relationships — tend to offer the most neutral read on where the company stands in the market. Ask them directly what your reputation is, when the market turns toward you and when it turns away, and how your leadership team is perceived. If they aren’t familiar with the brand at all, that’s useful information too. Sales teams in particular tend to trade in this kind of intelligence, so it’s worth building a relationship with them early.
Conversations with recently exited clients and recently lost pitches can be uncomfortable, but they’re often the most revealing. Ask open-ended questions, avoid leading the witness, and cast a wide net.
Set aside office hours where any employee can reach out directly. Enthusiasm is often the earliest signal of a change agent, and new CEOs need to find the people who will carry energy and momentum through the organization.
Gathering information is a critical step, but knowing how to read it matters more. A few things to watch for as the picture comes together:
The faster-horse instinct: Notice where executives and employees are more attached to the familiar process than to the outcome it’s supposed to produce; that reluctance is often mistaken for institutional knowledge.
Patterns over volume: Weigh the themes that repeat across departments more heavily than whatever the loudest person in the room is pushing.
Cross-functional friction: Ask specifically where the bottlenecks occur and where processes break down for customers. Friction between teams is usually a structure problem — think unclear ownership or roles — not a personality one.
A scorecard, not a gut check: Build one early, based on real KPIs, and go in with a hypothesis to test rather than a blank slate.
Earned authority: People follow leaders they trust. Restructuring or criticizing too early risks alienating a team before you’ve proven you understand their day-to-day work.
Go in with optimism — that energy is real, and people can feel it. Above all, close the loop and tell the organization what you heard and what you learned.
None of this replaces the strategic work ahead; the plan, the team, the go-to-market decisions still must get made. But that work is only as good as the picture it’s built on, and the picture is only as complete as the range of people a new CEO is willing to sit down with.
According to Equilar, between 12 and 13% of CEOs are new to their position in any given year, and roughly 28% of those new CEOs are being hired externally. That means a significant share of CEOs start the job with no real feel for the culture they’ve inherited, let alone the strengths and weaknesses of the business itself.
The first 60 days are when that gap either closes or hardens into blind spots that can follow a CEO for years, making this early window of listening and assessment one of the highest-leverage periods in the entire tenure.
Yet new CEOs often overlook key stakeholders during onboarding, and that mistake could mean missing out on crucial information that should inform strategic planning, the skills and structure required to build a leadership team and how a company refines its messaging and go-to-market approach.

20,2026-09-28,"The company claims 2M monthly active users who use the platform to find millions of jobs missing from the big job boards. Spark Capital led the $6.8M pre-seed round.
HiringCafe’s AI Talent Agent.
The modern job search has devolved into a notoriously soul-crushing endeavor. Candidates fire hundreds of resumes into the void. They navigate an endless stream of expired ‘ghost jobs’. And they compete against automated mass-application bots for a fraction of the actual open roles.
It’s a frustrating, high-friction loop. Legacy job boards, built primarily around employer advertising models, have done little to solve it.
Now, a startup born from a viral spreadsheet is aiming to rebuild the job hunt from the ground up.
HiringCafe, an agentic job discovery platform, announced that it has raised $6.8 million in pre-seed funding. The funding will expand its real-time career search engine. Spark Capital led the round. Nonfiction Capital, Silicon Gardens, and strategic angel investors hailing from legacy incumbents ZipRecruiter and Indeed participated.
Alongside the funding, the company is taking the wraps off its new AI Talent Agent. It is a 100% free tool. It allows job seekers to deploy personalized AI to scour millions of open roles on their behalf.
The core thesis behind HiringCafe is that traditional job boards are fundamentally misaligned with the needs of candidates. Legacy platforms rely heavily on companies paying to promote their listings. Because of that, they surface only a sliver of the actual hiring market. This dynamic forces millions of applicants into a hyper-competitive bottleneck. They aggressively fight over the same sponsored listings.
“We realized that the modern job hunt is fundamentally broken because legacy platforms operate on an advertising model, not a true search model,” Hamed Nilforoshan, co-founder of HiringCafe, explained. “They only show you a fraction of jobs that companies pay to promote, which creates hyper-competition and widespread burnout. By using AI to index the entire long tail of the job market, we are finally bringing the other jobs out of the dark and leveling the playing field for everyday job seekers.”
Rather than waiting for employers to upload and sponsor roles, HiringCafe operates more like Google. It uses proprietary web-scraping technology and internet-wide search algorithms. The platform bypasses aggregators entirely. It directly indexes the career pages of millions of companies in real-time. Currently, HiringCafe claims to host over 5.7 million active job listings. That is up to 400% more than some of the most popular mainstream job boards.
HiringCafe is launching an AI Talent Agent to help users parse this massive, uncurated index. It is designed to act as a 24/7 personalized recruiter.
Instead of doom-scrolling through listings and tweaking keywords, users simply upload their resume. The AI instantly parses the candidate’s granular skills and experience. It then generates highly targeted role recommendations. Crucially, the agent operates around the clock. It continuously scouts HiringCafe’s real-time index to flag new opportunities the moment they go live on a company’s site.
The platform is also taking direct aim at one of the biggest pain points in the current market: ghost jobs. The company says it engineered its platform to aggressively filter out stale, fake, or permanently “open” listings. Companies leave these up to project false growth. The goal is that candidates are only applying to legitimate roles. Many new AI-powered career tools gate features behind premium subscriptions. HiringCafe’s agent is entirely free for job seekers.
HiringCafe’s origin story is a classic Silicon Valley tale of scratching a personal itch. In 2022, co-founder Ali Mir left his job to build an HR startup. Shortly after, a wave of macroeconomic headwinds triggered massive layoffs across the tech sector. To help out his newly unemployed friends, Mir built a simple, curated spreadsheet of 10,000 hard-to-find startup jobs.
That spreadsheet struck a nerve. It went repeatedly viral across social media, racking up millions of views. It starkly highlighted the pent-up demand for a better way to find work. Nilforoshan was pursuing a PhD at Stanford at the time. He saw the immense real-world impact of Mir’s tool and paused his studies to join him.
Without a dime spent on marketing, the duo managed to grow HiringCafe to over 2 million monthly active users. Word-of-mouth and a fiercely dedicated community on Reddit fueled that growth entirely.
That organic, product-led growth is exactly what caught the attention of venture capitalists.
“Ali and Hamed have the hallmark of great founders: a simple vision that seems obvious the moment they say it,” said Fraser Kelton, General Partner at Spark Capital. “HiringCafe is building the absolute best product for people looking for a job, not for people advertising jobs. Their growth so far, without any marketing spend, is a testament to how much people want a product genuinely focused on helping them find one.”
With fresh capital and an AI agent now deployed, HiringCafe faces the formidable challenge of taking on entrenched giants like LinkedIn and Indeed. Millions of applicants are currently staring down the black hole of the modern job application process. For them, a search engine built entirely for the candidate might be exactly what the market ordered.

Home > Electric Vehicles
The electric SUV is set to make its full debut in early October before arriving in the US next year.
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars
Senior Writer, Electrified Cars
September 28, 2026, 4:51 pm ET
German automaker Volkswagen gave us our first camouflaged look at the newest member of its electric vehicle lineup on Monday morning. Dubbed the ID Tiguan, the compact electric SUV is set to replace the current VW ID 4 EV, with an “early 2027” launch in Europe and a US arrival later.
The ID Tiguan follows the ID Polo and ID Cross as the third electric vehicle in VW’s global portfolio to be renamed and brought in line with the brand’s combustion and hybrid models. Volkswagen says the rename — basically calling it an electric variant of the familiar Tiguan — makes it easier for customers to understand the vehicle’s size and capabilities, and to set their expectations, than the abstract numeral 4 does. I’m inclined to agree.
VW previewed the EV in yellow camouflage, hinting at proportions and details that are, unsurprisingly, similar to those of the current-generation Tiguan. The headlamps and light signature — visible through small cutouts in the camo — appear sharper and more angular, while the tail lights have a more geometric appearance than the combustion car. It’s safe to assume a touch more care has been paid to aerodynamics, since EVs generally need smaller grilles than combustion cars, but the overall shape and sculpting are largely spot-on to the Tiguan you can find at dealerships today.
More from CNET
Two versions of the ID Tiguan appear in the preview: likely a standard configuration and a sporty variant with unique wheels and what appears to be the Tiguan’s more aggressive R-Line bumper. On the combustion model, this indicates either the R-Line styling package or a higher-powered Tiguan Turbo spec. 
If this is the latter, I’m curious whether VW will use the “ID Tiguan Turbo” name for the hot crossover. Yes, I know that EVs don’t use turbochargers, but the automaker has told me previously that its use of “Turbo” on the combustion Tiggy denotes increased performance, not the presence of a turbocharger. It could apply that same logic to naming the EV. (Likewise, VW’s cousin, Porsche, has called its high-performance electric sedan the Taycan Turbo for years, despite it also lacking a turbocharger, so a precedent has been set.)
The ID Tiguan will replace the ID 4 in the US and will be sold alongside the combustion and hybrid-electric Tiguan models. VW hasn’t released any details on the ID Tiguan’s electric powertrain, performance or range, but the current ID 4 offers a decent baseline for speculating about improvements. Expect more concrete specs when the ID Tiguan makes its world premiere in “early October,” according to the release.
A VW representative told CNET via email that the brand would share North American timing and additional market-specific details “following its European launch in early 2027.” So, it may be some time before details of the US rollout solidify. I’ve a hunch that VW is waiting to see what happens with the US EV market over the next few months before committing to a plan.
VW isn’t the only traditional automaker rechristening its EVs. Mercedes-Benz’s next generation of electric vehicles will largely drop the EQx naming (EQE, EQA, EQG, etc.) in favor of names that align with the brand’s traditional “Class” names of its combustion cars (C-Class, CLA-Class, G-Class and so on), using the “with EQ Technology” suffix to denote a battery-electric powertrain.

22,2026-09-28,"At Jane Street, even picking T-shirts requires top talent.
Jane Street, the secretive trading quant firm, is hiring a ""Swag Program Manager,"" with a base salary of $150,000 to $200,000, plus an annual bonus, according to a job posting.
The well-compensated swag maestro will support the firm's recruiting and culture initiatives, the posting says, and contribute to both day-to-day operations and strategy. In the role, they'll help develop new products ""that feel current and true to the brand,"" and manage vendor relationships.
Jane Street is, as usual, looking for the best. The ideal candidate is an analytical-creative hybrid, with more than seven years of experience, a proven track record of developing products, and ""working knowledge"" of production and decoration mechanics, including embroidery and screen printing. They should also be comfortable building models and forecasts based on multiple data sources; the job posting doesn't specify why, but says the program manager needs to be comfortable ""defining success metrics.""
The job posting also hints at Jane Street's ambitions, since the employee will be responsible for ""improving processes as the program scales."" Jane Street, which was founded by a small group of traders in 2000, now employs more than 3,000 people. A representative for Jane Street declined to comment. 
Though some on X were surprised by the six-figure pay package for the swag manager position, the salary range is low for Jane Street, where entry-level quant traders can make $300,000 before bonus. In 2025, the average compensation per employee reached $2.7 million, according to Bloomberg.
Swag isn't new to the firm, or tech and finance companies more broadly. Firms can use merchandise to telegraph their identities or as a recruiting tactic, which is especially important as they vie for the top technical talent to stay competitive in AI. (Jane Street has invested in AI companies and says it has tens of thousands of its own GPUs.)
There's a niche corner of TikTok focused on ""Jane Street unboxing,"" full of t-shirts with hidden math problems. One video shows a merch box packed with a tote bag, shirt, pack of cards, Owala water bottle, and notebook, all branded with the firm's concentric-circle logo.

23,2026-09-28,"Three years ago, Adam Levinson's Singapore-based asset manager was the poster boy for the bond-market wreckage caused by Silicon Valley Bank's collapse.
Graticule Asset Management Asia, which started 2023 with roughly $3 billion, saw its flagship macro hedge fund lose more than 25%, mostly in the days following the bank's failure, thanks to wrong-way bets on short-term interest rates. The firm shut down the strategy in the first quarter of 2023 and returned capital to investors.
Now, Graticule is once again managing $3 billion, a person close to the firm told Business Insider, having rebuilt its assets under management to where they were prior to the collapse.
Instead of a hedge fund, the firm is managing capital for backers via separately managed accounts, this person said, including a macro strategy that is up 28% this year through the end of August. The average macro fund, according to hedge fund research firm PivotalPath, is up less than 6% through the same period.
SMAs are an increasingly common way for allocators to access alternative investment strategies, as the structure provides greater transparency into the underlying strategy than a traditional hedge fund. For aspiring asset manager founders, it can be a simpler way to launch a new firm or strategy.
The firm has also ""streamlined"" head count by using AI agents to assist investment staff, the person close to the firm said, reducing costs.
Regulatory filings show that the firm has 18 total staffers, including investment talent in offices in New York and Los Angeles, as well as at its headquarters in Singapore. At the start of 2023, regulatory filings show that the firm had 55 staffers.
Levinson is well-known in the Asia investing scene. A former Goldman Sachs prop trader, he was an executive at Fortress before spinning out and starting Graticule in 2015.

Training the robots' models in a simulation before training them in the real world won't make them safer. In fact if anything it will make them less safe due to their earliest training being subtly different from reality. What it does is make it cheaper and less dangerous to train them, and faster because you can run the simulation faster than reality.
This is laughably unscalable and noisy with only static models and zero useful topology or anything except that it looks halfways decent when covered with motion blur. Anything in a still falls apart after a bit because they just keep ignoring physics and insisting on having it both slow and imprecise, when you would rather have a tradeoff between speed and accuracy... I guess glossing it with photorealism helped, along with probably some insider deals.

28,2026-09-28,"Can you believe that Warner Bros. once shelved ""Coyote vs. Acme,"" a movie that (checks notes) argues that monopolistic corporations are not to be trusted and need to be actively kept in check? Yeah, in hindsight, it's not exactly shocking that notorious Warner Bros. CEO and sworn enemy of the Animaniacs David Zaslav felt comfortable about turning this highly entertaining live-action/animated ""Looney Tunes"" feature film into a glorified tax write-off. Thankfully, Ketchup Entertainment eventually swooped in to save the day by releasing ""Coyote vs. Acme"" in theaters. Now, you can either see what all the fuss is about for yourself or revisit it at home, as the flick will become available to watch on digital beginning September 29, 2026.
Directed by Dave Green (who also helmed the somewhat under-appreciated ""Earth to Echo"" and ""Teenage Mutant Ninja Turtles: Out of the Shadows""), ""Coyote vs. Acme"" ... does what it says on the tin. Namely, the movie follows Wile E. Coyote as he files a lawsuit against the nefarious Acme Corporation, which has spent decades supplying the clever canine with faulty gadgets that often literally blow up in his face when he tries to use them to finally catch that doggone Road Runner. What follows is a verifiably funny cross between a ""Who Framed Roger Rabbit""-style story that depicts cartoon characters as marginalized individuals in human society and a David vs. Goliath legal picture in the manner of the Oscar-nominated Paul Newman classic ""The Verdict"" or ""Erin Brockovich.""
Thanks to a witty script by Samy Burch (""May December""), itself partly inspired by the 1990 The New Yorker magazine article of the same name by Ian Frazier, ""Coyote vs. Acme"" makes for a fine addition to the Looney Tunes pantheon. But even if it had sucked, it still would've deserved to see the light of day, as the movie itself more or less argues with the anti-corporate malfeasance message at its core. That it's also full to the brim with inspiredly zany comedy, dynamic visuals that blend cartoon and real-world physics in playful and imaginative ways, and a narrative that's ultimately quite touching (with Wile E. Coyote, as always, serving as a metaphor for the indomitable human spirit in the face of our Sisyphean existence)? As the Road Runner would put it: ""Beep beep!""
Circling back to the film's digital rollout, you'll be able to either buy or rent it at the usual going rates from services like Fandango, Prime Video, and Apple starting September 29. There's no word just yet on its physical media release or streaming debut, but if the saga of ""Coyote vs. Acme"" has taught us anything, it's that good things come to those who are patient. That and those who vocally campaign against the forces in this world that value profits over everything else."
29,2026-09-28,"Since 2 August, European law has required systems that interact directly with people to say at the outset that they are an AI, on pain of fines reaching 3% of worldwide turnover, and that duty reaches providers established outside the union
Artificial Intelligence Chip
Instinct has raised $1B at a $10B valuation from Sequoia, Benchmark and Coatue for a personal AI agent that books trips, orders groceries and telephones businesses using its own phone. Since 2 August, Article 50 of the EU AI Act has required systems that interact directly with people to tell them they are dealing with an AI.
Instinct has raised $1B at a $10B valuation in a Series C from Sequoia Capital, Benchmark Capital and Coatue, Bloomberg reported. Last month the company was reported to be raising at $2.5B. The price has quadrupled in about four weeks.
The agent makes phone calls.
It plans trips, books reservations and orders groceries, and it has its own phone and computer, which it uses to ring businesses directly. Its agents can also talk to one another to coordinate plans and exchange files for users. Noah Shinn founded the company.
Europe has a rule for that.
Since 2 August, Article 50 of the AI Act has required providers to design systems that interact directly with people so that those people are informed they are dealing with an AI, unless it is obvious to a reasonably well informed person. The disclosure must come at the start of the interaction, clearly and distinguishably. Fines reach EUR 15M or 3% of worldwide turnover.
It binds companies outside Europe.
The obligation applies to providers established in third countries as well as in the union, and a limited grace period runs only to 2 December, and only for marking obligations on systems already on the market. Instinct has not said whether it operates in Europe. The rule does not depend on whether the person called is the customer.
Meta has the same product.
Muse books, buys and negotiates in much the same way and has been the closest comparison since it launched. Bloomberg notes that Meta has seen strong momentum with it recently.
Its calls were not all machines.
Reuters found Meta testing human contractors to place Muse’s calls, with the option enabled for half its employees and an opt-out available, we reported last week. A spokesperson said it would roll out with the proper disclosures and would not say what those are. Internal posts raised concerns that sensitive information could reach call-centre contractors.
The disclosure runs both ways.
One product must now tell the person answering that it is a machine, and the other has at times not been one. Europe wrote the first rule seven weeks ago and has nothing specific for the second. A restaurant taking a booking has no way to tell which it is speaking to.

30,2026-09-28,"Cloudflare chief executive Matthew Prince told The Verge that bots could make 1,000 times more web traffic than humans within five years. He wants AI agents to pay for what they read.
Cloudflare chief executive Matthew Prince at Web Summit 2025 in Lisbon
Bots could make up 1,000 times more web traffic than humans within five years, Cloudflare chief executive Matthew Prince told The Verge. He made the forecast on Nilay Patel’s Decoder podcast. He added that each of his earlier predictions on bot traffic had been wrong.
They were wrong because bots grew faster than he expected. At Web Summit last November, Prince said automated traffic would pass human traffic in the second half of 2027. By March, Cloudflare’s data pointed to the first half of 2027. In May, his team told him it had already happened.
“Five years from now, we think that automated traffic will be 1,000 times human traffic online,” Prince said.
Human traffic will stay roughly flat, he said. The growth comes from AI scrapers and agents. Cloudflare sits in front of more than 20% of the web.
For about 30 years, the web’s business model has been advertising, Prince said. That model does not work for bots.
“Bots don’t click on ads,” he said.
Cloudflare is working with Coinbase and Stripe to revive HTTP 402, a “payment required” code from the web’s early days. Site owners could charge AI agents a fraction of a penny for each page they access. Valuable content could cost more. Prince said AI companies on the buying side and big publishers on the selling side are both willing.
Patel pushed back on whether such a market would give people a reason to publish on the web. Yahoo chief executive Jim Lanzone said last week micropayments will never be enough for publishers.
From 15 September, Cloudflare set its free customers to block Google from using their sites for AI training by default. Site owners can switch the setting off.
“If they won’t differentiate between AI training and search engine training, we’re just going to block them across the board,” Prince said.
He said Google has committed to more transparency about its crawler. After a ruling in the UK, Google signalled that its crawler would say what it is doing on each page, Prince said. Publishers could then choose, and Google would apply this beyond the UK. Brussels is also asking publishers whether Google’s AI opt-out works.
Prince and his wife own the Park Record, a local newspaper in Park City, Utah. He expects it to make more from AI licensing deals than from digital advertising this year. In AI licensing, Reddit earns seven to 14 times more per token than The New York Times, he said.

31,2026-09-28,"Manfred Harrer, the Porsche and BMW engineer now running Hyundai's research, expects halo cars with solid-state packs by 2031 but does not believe the raw material cost can be solved in that time, while LG Energy Solution puts electric cars a decade out
A Hyundai dealership in St-Jean-sur-Richelieu, Quebec.
Hyundai research chief Manfred Harrer says carmakers cannot give up on solid-state batteries and expects small-volume halo cars within five years, but does not believe the raw material cost problem can be solved in that time. LG Energy Solution puts solid-state in electric cars roughly a decade away, and France is already subsidising a solid-state plant at Dunkirk.
Carmakers cannot give up on solid-state batteries, Hyundai’s research and development chief Manfred Harrer told reporters last week, InsideEVs reported. He expects small-volume halo cars with solid-state packs within five years. He does not expect the raw material costs to fall in that time.
He is a Porsche engineer.
Harrer spent his career at Porsche and BMW before Hyundai appointed him research and development chief in December. He calls solid-state the big bet in the industry right now. Hyundai is developing cells internally and with Solid Power, because it wants to own the technology rather than buy it.
The cost line is the story.
Everything can be solved in the end, Harrer said, “but it comes with cost, tremendous cost.” He does not believe the raw material problem can be solved five years from now. That keeps solid-state away from ordinary cars for considerably longer than the halo cars suggest.
Others put it later still.
LG Energy Solution’s North America president said in August that solid-state will reach phones about a decade before electric cars, and that large formats are where manufacturers struggle. The company is developing manganese-rich lithium-ion with General Motors instead, promising over 400 miles of range by 2028. The two forecasts are about five years apart.
France has already paid.
ProLogium broke ground in February on a plant at Dunkirk, beside the Gravelines nuclear station, for all-solid-state lithium ceramic cells measured at 381 Wh per kg by TUV Rheinland. Its existing Taiwanese line runs at about 0.5 GWh a year, or roughly 6,000 packs. The French plant is meant to start at 4 GWh.
Europe has done this before.
Northvolt was meant to be the continent’s answer to Asian cell making, and it collapsed after $15B of financing, having aimed at a quarter of the European market by 2030. Asian suppliers still hold 90% of it. The second attempt is a chemistry nobody has yet built at automotive scale.
Nobody has shipped one yet.
Demonstration cars exist, including a Mercedes running Factorial cells and a Ducati carrying QuantumScape’s. None of them is a car anyone can buy. Toyota has been working on solid-state for at least two decades.

32,2026-09-28,"Gregory Gibson is an IT specialist and technical writer who focuses on Linux and Windows Servers, Android customization, virtual private servers, web servers, and cybersecurity. 
Gregory has worked in enterprise IT for over 15 years and enjoys creating content to help people get the most out of their homelab and self-hosted IT projects, and to stay safe online. 
When not working, he enjoys photography, writing science fiction, and digital art.
My Galaxy Note20 Ultra is a 2020 flagship smartphone I actually bought as “old stock” back in November 2024. I’m still on the fence about whether to trade in its big screen, S Pen, awesome camera, and expandable storage for a newer phone within my budget.
Unfortunately, this phone is starting to show its age, with a single charge only lasting two-thirds of a day on its stock software. I did try to rescue it by flashing it with ArtisanROM, which dramatically improved battery life, but it left me wondering whether the battery itself had given up.
Would replacing it buy this phone some more time, or was I wasting money throwing it at an aging flagship? Samsung’s stock software declared the battery was “Good,” without telling me how much capacity actually remained. So I tested it four different ways before deciding whether to replace the battery or the phone.
The stock diagnosis and customer support app ""Samsung Members"" gave me an easy but not overly helpful result at first. Its battery diagnostic turned “Normal” and “Life: Good.” I ran it again after installing it on ArtisanROM and got back the same ambiguous verdict.
That was briefly reassuring until I looked at the line beneath it. “Capacity: 4500 mAh (typical)” describes the Note20’s original battery specification, but it was very unlikely it still held that much.
The battery information page in Settings gave me two numbers that didn’t really line up:
The discrepancies between these dates were, in part, explained by the phone’s weird history. The battery was manufactured on the 4th of July 2020, while the first-used date was the 4th of November 2024. That’s a four-year gap, and it does match because the phone was bought as an old stock item around September 2024.
Regardless of the results Samsung gave me, neither could actually tell me what the long shelf life or those 1,444 cycles had actually done to this battery.
Here, I was deciding between getting a replacement battery and a new phone. The “Good” result and the reported 91% were an ideal starting point, but I needed to watch the phone discharge and charge before trusting either number with that decision.
 Having the battery just sitting on the desk is one metric, but I wanted something closer to real-life use. I installed Battery Monitor from F-Droid, unplugged the phone at 44%, and let it play what I assumed was a very informative, hour-long nature documentary while I went and ate dinner.
Its history logged 43% at 8:02 pm and 34% at 8:56 pm, a nine-point drop in 54 minutes. The level stepped down one percent every few minutes. There weren’t any real sudden plunges or shutdowns.
This information was a bit more useful than simply calling the battery “Healthy.” Aging lithium-ion cells can lose capacity and, when worn down, struggle to maintain their voltage under load and start having performance problems. Samsung itself lists behavior like abrupt drain and unexpected shutdowns among the warning signs.
My video wasn’t exactly what I’d call a stress test, and I didn’t run it into the more revealing low-battery range. Still, sustained playback at mid-range charge behaved relatively normally.
The runtime forecasts were, unfortunately, really inconsistent. Battery Monitor later predicted 7 hours and 34 minutes remaining at 32%, while Samsung confidently predicted 15 hours and 31 minutes at 31%. Both were forecasting future use and not measuring battery health.
Samsung’s battery page also attributed 12.3% to YouTube over its displayed usage period. That figure shouldn’t be confused with the 9% lost in my timed test.
It was relieving, especially given the same test would have dropped the battery by ~20–30 on the stock software. However, I wasn’t ready to call the battery healthy just yet. ArtisanROM likely reduced the power the phone uses during ordinary tasks. The battery hadn’t magically regained lost capacity.
This test showed me the phone was working well enough to keep using — at least with the custom ROM. A proper measured test, though, would tell me if the battery needed replacing.
Battery Monitor tracks charge level, temperature, voltage, and battery history. Its discharge log helped me see how steadily my phone lost power during the video test.
 I installed AccuBattery from the Google Play Store because it demanded a substantial charging session before it would produce test results. At 28%, I set its design capacity to 4,500 mAh and, being impatient, connected my USB-C PPS charger.
The app showed live current and voltage, then tracked the climb to 100%. All up, it took 1 hour and 11 minutes, which was significantly less painful than watching the phone discharge.
Across those 72 percentage points, AccuBattery recorded 2,469 mAh added. Its estimated full capacity therefore was 3,429, since 2,469 divided by 0.72 is 3,429.11. Compared with the original 4,500 mAh specification, the battery has fallen a long way to a measly 76% health.
The phone’s battery information page claimed it was 91% healthy, and this was a 15-percentage-point difference that couldn’t just be ignored.
It’s incredible how a phone can last through a one-hour video while two battery tools completely disagree about how much of its original capacity can be salvaged.
This was the first result that made me start considering replacing not only the battery, but also the phone. It was clear that Samsung’s “Good” verdict was rather generous, but I wasn’t quite ready to declare it wrong because one test hardly qualifies as conclusive.
AccuBattery’s documentation states that it needs a 60% charge for its health calculations, and that multiple sessions improve its accuracy. My 72-point charge definitely qualified, but later charges could change the estimate completely.
The app’s “0.97 cycles” of wear was another issue. That’s a description of its wear estimates for this session, not the phone’s whopping 1,444 lifetime cycles. For now, 76% gave me a reason to keep measuring before finally deciding to scour Amazon.com for a replacement.

 Four checks later, I think Samsung’s “Good” was far too generous to be useful. AccuBattery estimated 76% health against 91% given by Samsung’s battery information. The issue is that the lower figure only came from a single charging session. I’ll need to keep measuring over the next few charges to see just how far off Samsung’s estimate was.
I’ve decided to replace the battery anyway. After 1,444 heroic full charge cycles, the lower estimate gives me enough reason to spend the money on a new cell. Outside of curiosity, I don’t really need to know its exact remaining capacity to make that choice.
Even though I know the Note20 Ultra won’t last forever, I still love using it. Its screen, camera, S Pen, and expandable storage, plus the improved performance running on ArtisanROM, are all features that I would have to check carefully for a potential replacement in 2026.
A new flagship of this caliber costs a lot more than the Note20 did back in 2024, while a phone within my budget range would mean giving up the things I love about this one. That’s a lot to sacrifice when a new battery just might give this awesome phone a few more good years.
