51,2026-09-28,"If you’re rocking an iPhone 16 or newer, you might be interested in a Qi2.2 charger to take full advantage of the 25W MagSafe charging speeds that your phone can support. Anker’s new 25W Prime MagSafe 3-in-1 is one of many great options, and it comes in an excellent portable design.
The big trend of the latter half of 2025 was bringing 25W Qi2.2 to every single wireless charging accessory possible, and that’s continued through 2026. Anker is kicking things off with a new portable MagSafe 3-in-1 that packs 25W Qi2.2 support into a very portable design.
With 25W of wireless charging, active cooling is practically a necessity. Nearly every 25W charger on the market integrates it, and the new Anker foldable 3-in-1 is no exception.
In the box, you get a 45W charge brick and USB-C cable – providing ample power to charge your iPhone, AirPods, and Apple Watch all at once. With this stand, you can charge an iPhone 18 Pro to 50% in just 27 minutes.
Remember MagSafe Duo? If you were someone who really enjoyed that product before Apple killed it off, you’ll likely enjoy this one. It has extremely similar portability characteristics, but comes with two key benefits: it can serve as a stand, and it can charge three devices at once instead of two.
One neat (though totally unnecessary) feature on this charger is that the Apple Watch charger is spring-loaded. It does fold inwards while you have the charger compacted, but when you’re ready to unfold everything, you just need to press downward on it and it springs outward. I thought that was pretty neat, and helps justify the premium price tag that this product demands.
All in all, if you want a nice MagSafe 3-in-1 charger that can easily work for travel, this new Anker offering is a great choice. Since it includes active cooling, it actually charges your iPhone rather quickly without any of the overheating or other negative side affects that wireless charging can sometimes have.
Anker’s new 3-in-1 MagSafe is available on Amazon with Prime delivery for just $99 currently, in both colors: black and white.

Rep. Tom Kean, Jr. and Rebecca Bennett sparred in the first and likely only debate between the two New Jersey candidates locked in one of the country’s most closely watched congressional races.
Kean, a Republican political scion whose father is a former Garden State governor, and Bennett, a former Navy helicopter pilot and first-time Democratic candidate, clashed on issues that ranged from President Donald Trump’s war in Iran, tariffs, how to fix healthcare costs, an ICE detention center in the district and their personal finances.
In his opening remarks, Kean said he had been an “effective legislator” during his time in Congress and the state legislature. He pledged to “challenge anyone” who got in his way of protecting New Jersey’s interests, including Trump.
Bennett opened by telling viewers “my life is the American dream.”
“ I earned an ROTC scholarship for my undergrad. I used the GI Bill for my MBA. My husband and I bought our house with a [U.S. Department of Veteran Affairs] loan,” she said.
Kean and Bennett are running in New Jersey’s 7th Congressional district, the state’s one true swing district this year, which stretches from the edge of Staten Island to the Pennsylvania border.
As a two-term incumbent, Kean has won fairly easily in the past. Republicans outnumber Democrats in the district by 20,000 registered voters. But a recent Stimsight Research poll gave Bennett a five point edge. And the Cook Political Report also recently moved the race from “Toss Up” to “Lean D.”
It was the first question of the evening. Moderator David Wildstein asked Kean if he made the “responsible” decision by not telling his constituents sooner about his mental health struggles, which kept him out of Congress for 116 days earlier this year.
Kean defended his decision not to disclose his depression diagnosis until he returned to the House of Representatives at the end of July.
“We were very clear with our constituents as well as the press that we were seeking medical treatment. And when things got updated over time, we kept everybody updated, and we were also very transparent when we spoke on the House floor about my condition of depression,” Kean said.
Bennett said she was happy Kean was getting the treatment he needed before shifting the discussion to Kean’s support for Trump’s “One Big Beautiful Bill” and its cuts to health care. State health officials have said that an estimated 350,000 New Jerseyans could lose health care due to cuts to Medicaid in the law.
She also criticized the legislation’s tax cuts for the wealthy and their impact on the country’s fiscal health. Multiple tax policy think tanks have estimated the law could add anywhere from $4 to $5 trillion to the national debt
”Congressman Tom Kean Jr., chose to saddle my daughters and the next generation with an insurmountable amount of debt to give tax breaks to the top 1% among us,” Bennett said number one is undoing the cuts that were done.
She said she would use the additional revenue to pay for programs like capping childcare costs for all families.
With Kean slumping in recent polls, and the president’s favorability rating dropping even further in the state, some New Jersey Republicans have urged Kean to distance himself from Trump. But Kean gave a robust defense of the president’s policies on Sunday night.
He called Trump’s tax and spending bill the “One big tax cut bill,” hailing it as the largest tax cut in American history and claiming the legislation “jumpstarted” the economy.
On the war in Iran, he said the president has taken a “clear and targeted approach” while lamenting past administrations’ diplomacy efforts in the Middle East.
Kean also said Trump’s use of tariffs was “justified,” despite concerns they are raising costs for average American households.
”The purpose of tariffs are to reduce the, not only to get leverage against a foreign entity, but also to make sure you lower the overall cost upfront. And that's what the tariffs are intended to do,” Kean said, adding that it was not Congress’s place to interfere with the president’s policy.
Bennett disagreed. She called for rolling back Trump’s tariffs.
“ Everything is more expensive: childcare, eldercare, housing, energy, groceries, healthcare. I could go on and on and on. And tariffs are directly impacting our cost of living,” Bennett said.
She also criticized Trump for having “no clear exit strategy” for the war in Iran, citing her over 15 years experience in the U.S military. And she said Congress “has not come close” to checking Trump’s power.
The two candidates sparred over Kean’s investment profits while in office.
Bennett accused Kean of being one of Congress’ “most  prolific stock traders.” In June, Gothamist reported that Kean profited by as much $340,000 from stock trades during his absence from Congress. Some of his trades have involved companies that play an integral role in the growth in AI data centers, which has been controversial in New Jersey and around the country,
Bennett said she and her husband have placed their investments in index funds and mutual funds.
“There are two ways to divest your personal interests from stocks. Number one is to do what I did, which is to divest all your individual stocks. Number two is to put your assets in an actual blind trust. My opponent has done neither of those things. And ultimately, his signature is on the form,” she said, referring to his signed personal financial disclosures to Congress.
Kean said he has nothing to do with the decisions around his personal trades.
“ I've got a blind structure in which I have no involvement and no input,” he said.
A debate moderator asked Bennett to disclose her net worth. Bennett appeared flustered by the question and did not provide an answer.
Kean estimated his net worth to be around $10 million.
Until now, Kean had been silent on the Trump administration plans to transform a warehouse in Roxbury, New Jersey into an ICE detention center. The warehouse is in Kean’s district and local Republican leaders in the township, along with many residents, have criticized him for not helping them oppose the detention facility.
Kean was asked Sunday if he could understand their frustration. He responded by saying he is against the administration’s plans, one of the few times he came out against one of Trump’s policies.
“ My focus is to make sure we don't have an ICE facility in Roxbury,” he said.
Bennett has spoken out against the project since February when the Department of Homeland Security purchased the 470,000-square-foot warehouse for nearly $130 million.
“ This is the first time we have heard Congressman Tom Kean Jr. actually be explicit about his position on the detention facility in Roxbury. So thank you for telling us where you stand finally,” Bennett said.
The candidates found more common ground during a lightning round of yes or no questions. Both agreed that Lake Ontario should be called Lake Ontario, and not Lake America as Trump has renamed it through an executive order in August. Neither would vote for Tucker Carlson or Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez for president, and both think pineapple on pizza is, well, fine.
“ I mean, if it's, like, the only pizza I'll eat it, but I wouldn't put it on there myself,” Bennett said.

WP Engine is gratified by the judge's ruling in its antitrust lawsuit. But Automattic also scored seven wins.
The judge in the WP Engine federal antitrust lawsuit ruled on motions to dismiss filed by Automattic, WooCommerce, and Matt Mullenweg, handing both WP Engine and the defendants significant victories and losses.
WP Engine won on most claims the defendants tried to dismiss, including its antitrust claims, but lost its claim that Automattic’s threats and computer interference amounted to illegal computer extortion. WP Engine failed to eliminate any counterclaim counts filed against it by the defendants; all seven survived at least in part.
U.S. District Judge Araceli Martínez-Olguín gave WP Engine favorable rulings in six parts of the motions to dismiss.
The judge ruled in WP Engine’s favor by keeping alive all four antitrust claims that the defendants had challenged, including monopolization, attempted monopolization, and the two illegal tying claims.
WP Engine’s claims of monopolization and attempted monopolization allege that Automattic and Mullenweg used control over the WordPress ecosystem to gain or maintain market power, restrict competitors, raise costs, and reduce competition. The judge found WP Engine had alleged enough market power, competitive harm, and anticompetitive conduct for those claims to continue.
The two illegal tying claims that survived the motions to dismiss allege that the defendants used control over access to WordPress.org to pressure users not to affiliate with WP Engine. One claim is under federal antitrust law and the other under California law. The judge found WP Engine had plausibly alleged this kind of negative tying, so both claims survived.
WP Engine’s three claims against WooCommerce also survived:
WooCommerce argued that WP Engine had grouped it together with the other defendants without identifying conduct that was specific to WooCommerce. The judge rejected that argument, pointing to a trademark-related letter sent by counsel representing WooCommerce and Automattic as well as an instance in which a WooCommerce employee solicited a WP Engine customer after WP Engine was blocked from accessing WordPress.org.
The judge concluded:
“These allegations do not, then, as WooCommerce contends, fail to “‘differentiate allegations against multiple defendants.'” “
The defendants had a big win because the judge allowed all seven of their counterclaims to survive at least in part. The claims accuse WP Engine of trademark infringement and dilution, misleading use of WordPress-related branding, false advertising, and unfair competition. The judge declined to dismiss them because many of WP Engine’s challenges could not be resolved at the motion-to-dismiss stage.
The judge explained in the court document:
“Whether the counterclaims are barred by laches, nominative fair use, or because the WordPress Marks were not famous, are factual issues not suitable for resolution at this stage.
…WP Engine’s resort to the statute of limitations as a basis for dismissal is also unavailing.”
The seven counterclaims that survive at least in partial form are:
A spokesperson for WP Engine shared this official statement:
“We are gratified by this important ruling allowing our antitrust claims to proceed, finding that we have sufficiently alleged market power, anticompetitive conduct, and market-wide harm to competition – not just harm to WP Engine.
At its core, this case is about how Automattic and Matt Mullenweg abused their power over the WordPress ecosystem to punish a competitor, suppress competition, increase costs, decrease quality, and undermine the open ecosystem that millions of customers and businesses rely on. We look forward to proving our many claims.”

54,2026-09-28,"When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here's how it works.
Premium compact cameras occupy an interesting space in the camera market. They are small enough to carry almost anywhere, but unlike basic point-and-shoot cameras, they can offer large sensors, fast lenses, advanced autofocus and controls aimed at photographers who want more than a smartphone can provide.
For enthusiast photographers, that combination can be extremely appealing. You get much of the creative control of a mirrorless camera without having to carry a camera body and several lenses everywhere you go.
The best premium compact camera depends on what you value most. Some prioritize image quality, others offer a versatile zoom, while fixed-lens models can provide an exceptionally simple shooting experience.
Affiliate links
The Sony RX100 VII remains one of the most versatile choices for photographers who want serious capabilities in a genuinely compact body.
Its built-in zoom is one of its biggest advantages. Instead of being limited to one focal length, you can photograph wider scenes and then zoom in when a subject is farther away.
That makes it particularly useful for travel, family photography, street scenes and everyday shooting.
The camera also offers sophisticated autofocus, which is useful when photographing people or moving subjects. For enthusiasts who don't want to carry interchangeable lenses, having this level of flexibility in a small body is a major benefit.
Photographers who want maximum versatility without changing lenses.
Compact body, useful zoom range, strong autofocus and capable photo and video performance.
The Fujifilm X100VI takes a different approach.
Rather than trying to cover every focal length, it combines a high-resolution APS-C sensor with a fixed 23mm f/2 lens, equivalent to 35mm on a full-frame camera.
That focal length is particularly useful for street photography, portraits, travel and everyday photography.
The fixed lens also encourages a more deliberate approach. Instead of zooming from a distance, you have to think about your position and composition.
Fujifilm's film simulations are another major attraction. They allow photographers to produce distinctive JPEG images directly from the camera, which can be useful when you want to spend less time editing.
Enthusiasts, street photographers and travelers who enjoy a fixed-lens shooting experience.
Large APS-C sensor, fast fixed lens, excellent image quality and distinctive JPEG color options.
The Ricoh GR IV is aimed at photographers who want maximum portability without giving up an APS-C sensor.
Its 18.3mm f/2.8 lens provides a 28mm-equivalent field of view, making it particularly well suited to street photography, travel and documentary-style shooting.
The camera is small enough to carry discreetly, which is one of its biggest strengths.
For photographers who spend hours walking through cities, markets and neighborhoods, that compact design can be more important than having a larger camera with interchangeable lenses.
The fixed lens does require a different way of shooting, but that's part of the appeal.
Street photographers and enthusiasts who prioritize portability.
Extremely compact APS-C design, fast wide-angle lens and stabilization.
Premium compact cameras are more than simply small cameras.
They typically offer larger sensors than basic point-and-shoot models, better lenses, more manual controls and more advanced autofocus.
The important thing is that you don't have to sacrifice portability to get those features.
For enthusiasts, a premium compact can make an excellent second camera. You might use a mirrorless system for planned shoots and keep the compact camera with you for everything else.
This is one of the biggest decisions you'll need to make. Neither approach is inherently better.
Premium compact cameras can use different sensor sizes, and that affects both image quality and camera design.
Larger sensors generally provide advantages in low-light shooting and depth-of-field control. However, a larger sensor can also require a larger lens and body.
That's why some compact cameras use smaller sensors while offering impressive zoom ranges. The resulting camera may be much easier to carry.
For enthusiasts who value portability, absolutely.
A smartphone may be more convenient, but a premium compact camera provides physical controls, dedicated lenses, larger sensors and a more traditional photographic experience.
It can also encourage you to take photography more seriously without requiring a complete interchangeable-lens system. The key is choosing the right type of compact camera for your shooting style.
An excellent choice for photographers who want maximum flexibility from a pocketable camera.
Particularly appealing if you enjoy street photography, fixed focal lengths and Fujifilm's distinctive image rendering.
Ideal for enthusiasts who want an exceptionally portable APS-C camera for everyday and street photography.
Premium compact cameras aren't designed to replace every mirrorless system. Instead, they offer something different: serious photographic capability that is small enough to take almost anywhere.
For an enthusiast photographer, that can be one of the most valuable features of all.

LG's 39GX950B-B is one of a kind, with exceptional visual quality ready for games, movies, and other entertainment. Unsurprisingly, its 5K2K resolution (5120x2160) does a lot of heavy lifting, but that's further complemented by its 39-inch display size, providing a higher 143 PPI (Pixels Per Inch) than its 45-inch counterpart, and its fantastic 4th-gen tandem OLED panel. It's very pricey, but that doesn't stop it from being one of the best gaming monitors money can buy.

Why you can trust TechRadar




We spend hours testing every product or service we review, so you can be sure you're buying the best. Find out more about how we test.

 



It's impossible to describe the LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B as anything short of phenomenal. It's the end goal for any PC gamer striving for the best quality, with its immersive 21:9 aspect ratio and crisp image quality, thanks to its 5K2K (5120x2160) display resolution.
It also uses a 4th-gen tandem OLED panel, best known for its improvements in sustaining high levels of brightness, reducing burn-in chances, and better efficiency, which is great for those long hours of gaming and productivity.
The 39GX950B-B has options to tackle the brightness uniformity and ABL (Automatic Brightness Limiter) frustrations that are common with OLED displays. In the monitor's OSD (On Screen Display), under 'brightness level', there are options for high and low levels as well as a 'uniform' setting that ultimately produces lower brightness, but sidesteps ABL issues.
Fortunately, though, I had little to no issues with ABL during gaming sessions, especially when testing Horizon Forbidden West, where HDR works best, and there are constant changes in scenery and color — but it's worth noting that it's more noticeable while working on a Windows desktop, where you may be opening and closing several different applications.
At 5K2K, you're essentially getting a standard 4K tandem OLED monitor that's widened to a 21:9 aspect ratio from 16:9. That doesn't come without downsides: 5K2K drives 33% more pixels than 4K, so using this display means you'll be better off using a powerful GPU (at least an RTX 4080 Super, RTX 5070 Ti, or higher) to hit higher frame rates without being overly reliant on upscaling.
While you might think the latter is solved if you already own a beefy GPU, 5K2K is effectively a permanent commitment (at least for the foreseeable future) to purchasing high-end GPUs, unless the generational leap in power for low- to mid-range GPUs increases sooner.
However, the 39GX950B-B features a 1080p Dual Mode (2580x1080 resolution), with a bump up to a 330Hz refresh rate, and an AI upscaling option within the display itself. The former is not only great for competitive gamers who prioritize high frame rates in online games, but it also ultimately reduces the heavy strain on the GPU.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
AI upscaling is a great bonus for anyone looking to drop the internal resolution, especially if console gaming is required, as the monitor detects a non-native input signal and upscales it to 5K2K. I didn't have much reason to use this since my RTX 4080 Super is much better off utilizing Nvidia's DLSS technology in games, but it's great that LG included this as an alternative.
Overall, the LG 39GX950B-B is almost unmatched at delivering the most immersive gaming experiences possible, with HDR brightness that can hit up to 1,500 nits (in small highlights), and visual fidelity that had me gobsmacked at the visual detail in every game and movie I tested. I say almost, for a good reason.
 



The biggest downside of the LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B is its price, starting at $1,799.99 / £1,599.99 / around AU$2,799. It essentially places this display out of reach for most PC gamers, especially when its only major competition, the Alienware AW3926QW, is much cheaper at $1,099 / £999 / AU$1,999.
It's made worse for PC gamers, who may need to upgrade their entire gaming PC or GPU, in a PC hardware market already in complete disarray during an ongoing RAM crisis, leading to exceedingly overpriced hardware.
For a system that can handle the 5K2K resolution, the entire setup (including the monitor) would likely cost over $2,000 / £2,000 / AU$2,840, so it's not exactly accessible, despite its high level of quality.
Before the Alienware AW3926QW entered the market for consumers, the 39GX950B-B was still an expensive purchase, but the presence of competition at a far more affordable price has slightly diminished the value of LG's display.
If LG can drop the RRP (Recommended Retail Price) closer to Alienware's, it will likely attract more buyers for this 5K2K monitor.

Much like fellow UltraGear Evo ultrawide displays, the 39GX950B-B features the same sturdy monitor stand, with excellent flexibility in swivel adjustments, as well as height and tilt. I often prefer a heavy-duty monitor arm for ultrawide monitors for more space, but I was reluctant to opt for one for my previous LG 45GS95QE 1440p ultrawide OLED, and I've kept the same trend with the 39GX950B-B.
Unlike its competition, the 39GX950B-B's screen has a matte finish and anti-glare design, and the latter has been fantastic when gaming or working in a bright, sunlit room. I prefer glossy screens, as they often provide an extra edge in image sheen and sharpness, evident in my review of the Alienware AW3426DW; however, some glossy screens (not Alienware's) can suffer from unwanted reflections in some scenarios, so I'm satisfied with the matte finish on this LG 5K2K monitor.
The OSD feels like a strong step up from non-UltraGear Evo models in terms of its style, with a new segment for an image to represent each category in settings, providing a premium look for a premium gaming monitor.
It's also great to see the 90W Power Delivery USB-C charging port, which is ideal for charging laptops or any separate devices used on a desktop.
I have my own PC speakers, so built-in monitor speakers aren't a focus at all for me, but unlike other monitors, the 39GX950B-B features two internal 7W speakers — and this was actually quite handy for me using my PS5 console (at a 16:9 aspect ratio) on the 39GX950B-B, since Sony's console can't use normal Bluetooth functionality on any audio device.
The only downside that's quite noticeable is the high temperatures felt after a few hours of use, which, in all fairness, should be expected for a monitor with 11 million pixels and 1,500 nits of peak brightness.
 



There is a wide range of features to utilize on the LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B, which is pivotal considering the 5K2K resolution's high demand on GPUs.
AI upscaling for both image and sound is available via the OSD, with the former notably useful for any user with a PC that might not be able to handle 5K2K. It works by dropping the display resolution on Windows (or Linux) and then enabling the mode with either the high or low setting.
Combining AI image upscaling with Dual Mode works wonders and is a great option for anyone looking to buy the 39GX950B-B and planning to upgrade their PC hardware to utilize the full 5120x2160 display resolution.
Dual Mode also allows 2560x1080 at 330Hz, which is a match made in heaven for competitive gamers, especially when taking advantage of upscaling via AMD's FSR or Nvidia's DLSS 4.5.
Brightness level options are high, low, and uniform. I found high to be the best setting to use for gaming, particularly games that are bright and colorful, and either low or uniform for movies and TV shows. Using high in movies or TV shows often made the ABL and dimming issues more noticeable, whereas uniform ensures brightness remains consistent.
 



The LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B absolutely knocks it out of the park in performance. The 4th-gen tandem OLED setup really shows its worth, with high levels of brightness in HDR across several games, inky-deep black levels thanks to the 1,850,000:1 contrast ratio, and exceptional image quality at 5K2K.
To really showcase the 39GX950B-B's standout performance, I played games like Control Resonant, Battlefield 6, and notably, Horizon Forbidden West, all of which are well-optimized PC ports and have well-implemented HDR.
Horizon Forbidden West looks phenomenal on this display, with its colorful environments and intense gameplay. Using DLSS 4.5 on my RTX 4080 Super, playing this game in either DLSS Balanced or Performance mode felt no different, as the image quality is at a new level entirely.
In case it wasn't clear enough, 5K2K is essentially a 4K monitor at ultrawide, so the benefits of the expanded field of view and increased immersion are still present, while sharpness in character model details and foliage take a huge leap.
I left Dynamic Tonemap on via the OSD for most of my game tests, including the high brightness level option, and found that HDR worked brilliantly in several scenes, even in the brightest scenarios. I realized that the detail that might usually be lost in bright sequences on older OLED models was maintained with the LG 39GX950B-B, and that's evident in the Forbidden West example above.
Before reviewing this model, I was accustomed to a 240Hz refresh rate with the LG UltraGear 45GS95QE OLED ultrawide, at a 3440x1440 display resolution. The fall to 165Hz with this UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B seems significant, but it wasn't noticeable for me or a concern at all, especially since the jump from 1440p on a 45-inch display to 2160p on a 39-inch monitor is massive. To clarify, that is going from 83 PPI to 143 PPI, providing better sharpness in every way.
Unlike the Alienware AW3926QW, the LG 39GX950B-B doesn't feature an RGB stripe subpixel layout; instead, it has a white subpixel layout, but honestly, text clarity wasn't an issue for me using LG's monitor — and frankly, that's due to the heavy lifting done by the 5K2K's pixel density.

You want the best ultrawide gaming monitor availableAlongside its Alienware AW3926QW competition, the LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B is one of the best ultrawide gaming monitors you can buy now, and frankly one of the best gaming monitors on the market. Its 5K2K resolution and 4th-gen tandem OLED panel setup provide an unbelievably pleasant sight to behold, giving games you've never felt like playing before an extra edge.
You want a monitor that doesn't destroy desk spaceIt's not a 34-inch ultrawide display, so it's not exactly small, but compared to its 45-inch counterpart, the LG 39GX950B-B is fantastic for preserving desk space. Its 39-inch size is the sweet spot for immersion, without feeling like a TV is on your desk.
You’re looking for an affordable 5K2K monitorThe LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B is a very expensive gaming monitor, one that many gamers will find hard to justify purchasing, despite all of its amazing features and performance. It's made worse since its main competition is available at a cheaper, more reasonable price tag.
You don't have a powerful GPUThe 5K2K display resolution has 33% more pixels than a standard 4K monitor, meaning the system requirements are significantly higher, especially if you're coming from a 1440p monitor. While mid-range GPUs can likely still run games at the 5K2K resolution, upscaling via DLSS, FSR, or even LG's monitor AI upscaling will likely be doing the heavy lifting for smooth performance.
Alienware AW3926QW
The Alienware AW3926QW is essentially in the same category as the LG UltraGear Evo 39GX950B-B, spec-wise, with the bonuses of its RGB stripe subpixel layout, Dolby Vision, and glossy screen (depending on preference). It's a much cheaper 5K2K OLED monitor, starting at $1,099 / £999 / AU$1,999, instead of the 39GX950B-B's $1,799.99 / £1,599.99 / around AU$2,799.
Read our full Alienware AW3926QW review
I used the LG UltraGear 39GX950B-B for two weeks for gaming, movies, and productivity. The games I tested include: Resident Evil Requiem, Dragon's Dogma 2, Control: Resonant, Battlefield 6, Crimson Desert, Horizon Forbidden West, Uncharted: Legacy of Thieves, and Final Fantasy 7 Remake Intergrade. Some of the movies include Avengers: Infinity War and Guardians of the Galaxy Vol. 2, and Blade Runner 2049.
While this display doesn't have the RGB stripe subpixel layout that its rival Alienware AW3926QW has, I had no issues with any form of text fringing or clarity, which I suspect is due to the pixel density at 5K2K.
ABL issues are always present on OLEDs in some form, but thanks to the 4th-gen tandem OLED, this wasn't noticeable in most gaming scenarios, and it comes with a solution: a uniform brightness option when watching movies or TV shows, where it can be slightly more obvious.

Every few months, I sit down to look back at the Mac threat landscape, and every time I expect to write about something new. This time I can’t. But that doesn’t mean nothing is happening. In fact, the opposite.
Since I published the Security Bite Q1 2026 review, we’ve seen ClickFix go from the new kid on the block and the technique to watch to now the default delivery method for almost every new Mac stealer. Threat actors are getting much better at using it too (I suppose the kids would call this “locked in”). Recently we’ve seen attackers implement persistence, backdoors, and even infrastructure that hides inside Apple’s own services.
Here’s what you should know as a security practitioner or a malware-fearing Mac owner…

To no one’s surprise, Apple’s 26.4 Terminal prompts didn’t slow this attack vector down.
Nearly every new Mac malware family from the past few months arrived the same way: a fake CAPTCHA or “fix” page that gets the victim to paste a command into Terminal themselves. ClickLock, discovered by Group-IB. A new Go-based stealer Huntress spotted in August. The latest MacSync variant, which Kaspersky found spreading through pages posing as Homebrew and a disk space cleanup tool. More on each soon.
What makes ClickFix so successful is that it doesn’t need to go up against Gatekeeper, notarization, XProtect, or any of the Mac’s incredibly capable antivirus tools. This is really because the user is coerced into infecting themselves by running the script. Apple’s warning prompts were a good first step, but attackers are scrappy and will always find ways around. Like Script Editor.
So, ClickFix is the delivery technique. Now let’s talk about the payload.
In its old school form, a Mac infostealer was very much a smash and grab. It would run once, dump your passwords and crypto wallets to something like a Telegram bot, and then vanish. That’s certainly not the case anymore. Just this year, we’ve seen examples like ClickLock that social engineer users into giving up their system password and leave a backdoor behind for round two.
MacSync, which came into the world as an AMOS lookalike, picked up a backdoor module too. Moonlock Lab put it best in its mid-year report, saying the 2026 model isn’t a stealer, it’s a persistent implant that happens to start with stealing.
Arguably, the more interesting change is how they’re hiding now.
MacSync’s latest version actually pulls its commands from a public iCloud calendar, so anyone watching network traffic just sees the Mac talking to Apple. CrashStealer, found by Jamf Threat Labs in July, posed as Apple’s own crash reporting framework inside a notarized app, so Gatekeeper just let it right through. Neither is a flaw in macOS. It’s attackers figuring out that the easiest way past Mac’s built-in defense mechanisms is to look like something trusted from Apple.
Good explanation of the iCloud calendar trick:
MacSync's iCloud Calendar trick is pretty cool. One MacSync downloader analyzed by Securelist contains a URL such as: https://caldav.icloud[.]com/published/…It retrieves a public .ics calendar, creates an anonymous pipe, launches zsh -s, sets the pipe as stdin, and feeds the… pic.twitter.com/TTVHfpj07O
There’s not much users can do beyond updating to the latest versions of the operating system. As of today, that means iOS 27 or 26.7 and macOS Golden Gate or Tahoe 26.7. I would also recommend turning off Screen Sharing if you don’t use it. And the big one: NEVER paste a command into Terminal given to you by a website or pop up alert.
Tibit if you’re defending a Mac fleet: the single most valuable stealer detection right now is Terminal, Script Editor, or osascript spawning from a browser. Pair it with alerts for curl or base64 piped directly into a shell because the first stage doesn’t go to disk. If you’re only scanning files, it’s too late.

A version of this recipe first appeared in The Bite, Salon's food newsletter. Sign up for early access to articles like this, plus recipes, food-related pop culture recommendations and conversations about what we're eating, how and why. 
For a while, I kept a soup journal.
Not a recipe journal, exactly. More like field notes. If I ate a soup I liked out in the world, Iâd jot down what was in it, what the broth tasted like, sometimes sketch the bowl. I just adore the form.
Especially when I lived alone, it was not unheard of for all three meals in a day to be soup. One particularly triumphant day started with congee in Washington, D.C., continued with enchilada soup at an airport Chiliâs and ended with pastina back home in my pajamas.
So, in honor of soup season, Iâve decided to spend the next few weeks of âThe Bite,â Salonâs food newsletter, teaching what Iâve started referring to in my notebook at Soup School: a loose little series about the patterns that make soup better. Weâll talk about the ingredient your soup is probably missing â vinegar! â plus better soup toppers and, eventually, how to host a soup party.
Related
But lesson one is the foundation for a lot of the soups I make at home: Start in the oven.
I donât mean every soup. Some soups depend on freshness, delicacy or a particularly clean broth, and Iâll get to those. But when I want depth, sweetness, browning and the kind of flavor that tastes like something has been cooking much longer than it actually has, I start by roasting.
Hereâs the method.
Tomato soup was the one that made this click for me.
Mine started with garlic, red onion, sun-dried tomatoes, cherry tomatoes, fennel, olive oil, spices and an anchovy or two, all piled into a Dutch oven and roasted together.
The oven transformed every ingredient differently. The onion and fennel softened, browned and grew sweeter. The anchovy sizzled itself into the oil coating those browned alliums. The cherry tomatoes burst, their skins turning silky and their juices almost pasty around the edges. The sun-dried tomatoes somehow became even more concentrated. The spices perfumed absolutely everything.
By the time the pot came out of the oven, most of the flavor was already there.
That, to me, is the whole appeal of the method. You can build a similar kind of depth on the stovetop, of course, but the oven does a lot of the work for you. Dry heat browns, evaporates and concentrates while you go do something else.
And when you roast, push things a little farther than may initially feel instinctive.
Your vegetables should not be pretty anymore. I want onions slumped and browned at the edges. Tomatoes wrinkled and burst. Mushrooms dark. Carrots blistered. Bits sticking to the bottom of the pot. If everything still looks ready for a glossy farmers market photo shoot, give it another few minutes. This works beautifully with fresh vegetables, but frozen vegetables are welcome, too. So are drained canned beans.Â 
Douse whatever you have in oil, season it well and let it get ugly.
We need your help to stay independent.
This is also when I deploy what I lovingly think of as my potions and powders: anchovy, tomato paste, miso, soy sauce, fish sauce, bouillon paste or cubes, mushroom and brown gravy powder.
Not all at once, obviously, though I respect the chaos.
The point is to let those deeply savory ingredients spend some time in the heat rather than simply dissolving them into broth at the end. Tomato paste darkens. Anchovy melts into the oil. Spices bloom. Everything has a chance to mingle before the soup gets diluted.
Once everything is properly roasted, I add just enough broth to loosen the browned, sticky bits from the bottom of the pot. Not a carton. Not even close. At this point, the broth isnât there to create the flavor. Itâs there to capture what the oven already made.
Scrape thoroughly. Those dark little bits on the bottom are part of the soup now.
For a lot of vegetable, bean and chowder-style soups, I blend about half of whatâs in the pot.That gives you body without turning the whole thing into purÃ©e. The blended vegetables thicken the broth, while the rest keep enough texture to remind you what youâre eating.
You can do this in a blender or with an immersion blender (though I tend to like the control of scooping part of the mixture into a blender and leaving the rest alone.)
Here is the part that feels a little counterintuitive: after going to the trouble of deglazing the pot, I often cook everything back down again. I let the blended and unblended mixture simmer until it gets thick, concentrated and almost sticky. Then I add my final stock, cream or coconut cream until the soup reaches the consistency I actually want.
This is one of my strongest soup opinions: donât drown the thing you just concentrated.
You can always add more broth. You cannot easily take it back out (though, if you do, itâs not the end of the world: just let the soup reduce longer.)Â 
Add a little. Stir. Taste. Add more if you need it.
Want more great food writing and recipes? Sign up for Salonâs free food newsletter, The Bite.
Once the texture is right, I finish with whatever gives the soup its final bit of life: herbs, citrus zest, salt, black pepper, maybe cream. Those ingredients donât need half an hour in the oven. They need to stay bright.
Once I noticed this pattern, I realized itâs how I make most of my favorite soups: tomato soup, corn chowders, vegetable soups, bean soups and even chicken soup.
For chicken soup, I roast thighs on a bed of onion, garlic, carrot and celery with big lemon slices, garlic powder, onion powder and plenty of black pepper. Once the chicken is cooked, I pull it out to shred later, while the vegetables underneath â softened in chicken fat, lemon and all those browned juices â become the base of the soup.
I am certainly not claiming to have invented roasted chicken soup. Italian penicillin nonnas have been several centuries ahead of me on that front. But the example stands.
Roast first. Deglaze. Blend some. Reduce. Reconstitute. Finish.
There are plenty of soups I would never subject to this treatment.
If I want something fresh, delicate or particularly clean-tasting, I stay on the stovetop. A clear miso broth with beautiful soft cubes of tofu doesnât need caramelization. Neither does a simple cream of potato or parsnip soup (more on those next week!), or a bright, stewy pozole verde.
My spring pea soup â peas, stock, cream, dill, black pepper and lemon zest â absolutely does not belong in the oven. She is delicate. Leave her alone.
But if the goal is depth, browning and concentration, this is the pattern I come back to over and over.
Take the vegetables you have. Fresh or frozen. Add some canned beans if youâve got them. Douse the whole situation in oil and spices and roast until things get a little ugly. Deglaze. Blend some of it. Reduce it. Add broth carefully. Finish it with whatever makes sense.
It is not a precise science. It is not particularly pretty.
It is, however, delicious.
This is a current variation on roasted tomato soup Iâve been making a lot recently: warmly spiced with garam masala, fennel and coriander, rounded out with miso and coconut cream. Garam masala is an Indian spice blend whose exact makeup varies, but it commonly includes warming spices like cumin, cardamom, cinnamon, cloves and black pepper.
Ingredients
2 large beefsteak tomatoes, roughly chopped
1 medium white onion, roughly chopped
4 to 5 garlic cloves
1 tablespoon minced fresh ginger
3 tablespoons olive oil
1 tablespoon white miso
1 tablespoon tomato paste
1Â½ teaspoons garam masala
1 teaspoon fennel seeds
1 teaspoon ground coriander, or lightly crushed coriander seeds
Â½ to Â¾ cup chicken stock, for deglazing, plus about 1 cup more to finish
â cup coconut cream, plus more for serving
Kosher salt and freshly ground black pepper, to taste
Sliced scallions, for serving
Fresh cilantro, for serving
Toasted bread, for serving
Â 
Directions
This story originally appeared in The Bite, my weekly food newsletter for Salon. If you enjoyed it and would like more essays, recipes, technique explainers and interviews sent straight to your inbox,Â subscribe here.

We’re not necessarily the next generation of Leonardo da Vincis. Not all of us can, will, or frankly should come up with new inventions or clever ideas to make life easier. Well, Shark Tank is proof of that.

But every once in a while, someone who’s particularly creative, tech-savvy, and, well, wealthy enough to own a good-quality 3D printer will take matters into their own hands and create everyday items that can make life a little easier for the rest of us. That’s the case with the person behind the @af_inventions Instagram account, who shares all kinds of clever little creations they’ve invented. And these are some of the coolest ones we’ve seen.
This post may include affiliate links.

These inventions were all made with 3D printers, and while they may seem like a modern tool for creating all kinds of things, the first commercially available 3D printer actually went on sale in 1987. It was the SLA-1, created by Chuck Hull and 3D Systems. But there’s a reason you didn’t see everyone using one: it cost over $100,000.
It was also strictly designed for industrial manufacturing, so it wasn’t exactly something you could pick up at your local tech store. But from 2007 onward, desktop 3D printers became more commonly available to the public. Companies like MakerBot started releasing affordable printer kits, allowing hobbyists and anyone interested in trying 3D printing to afford one; by 2012, prices dropped even further, making them an affordable option for the average consumer.

There are also plenty of printer types, some much easier to use than others. But generally, tech experts say modern 3D printers are fairly easy for beginners to use. Actually mastering one like the creators on this list, however, is where things get tricky. Some modern consumer printers already come fully assembled, with smart sensors and even pre-designed 3D models.
But you might still see immediate challenges, like calibrating the printer and adjusting temperatures, dealing with filament jams, and figuring out which materials to use, and how to use them. Plus, if you want to make your own custom objects, you normally have to learn computer-aided design software to create the designs, and some of these programs can also have a learning curve.

Nowadays, though, 3D printing goes well beyond expectations. In the medical world, especially, there have been many breakthroughs thanks to 3D printing, or more specifically, bioprinting. Specialists explain that by combining a patient’s stem cells, growth factors, and nutrients with hydrogel matrices, they can print fully functional biological structures layer by layer.
But what kind of biological structures? Well, there have been plenty. Among the clinical milestones, 3DBio Therapeutics bioprinted an ear for a patient born with microtia. It was a full ear implant made from her own cells, reducing the risk of rejection. Bone and cartilage reconstructions are also possible, and surgeons can print custom-shaped bones. Pretty cool.

Did you know houses have also been built using 3D printers? Obviously, we’re not talking about your average at-home printer, where you can easily print an Overwatch weapon model. Instead, architects can use giant robotic printers to build homes, schools, and even commercial structures from specialized concrete and recycled materials.
It supposedly takes only a few days, dramatically cutting construction costs and reducing material waste. This is especially interesting now that we have biocomposites made from hemp fiber and lime, known as hempcrete. However, if you’re in the US, you can only use this material as insulating infill around a load-bearing frame, so keep that in mind. For when you own a giant robot printer, of course.

Frankly, this tool is also used in many other areas, like haute couture, aerospace, and advanced engineering. Funnily enough, it is also often used in art preservation, which isn’t something one often considers. Apparently, many museums use 3D scanning and printing to replicate fragile historical artifacts.
This lets visitors handle replicas without damaging the original artifacts. 3D printing offers so many benefits that, honestly, if you’re not using it at an industrial level, the examples we brought today may be the inspiration you need for your next home project. So, do you have a 3D printer? If so, what has been your favorite thing to make? Let us know!

Miryam Naddaf is a reporter for Nature in London.

Search author on:

Welcome to the peer-review market, where third-party companies are advertising freelance reviewing jobs. Credit: Kseniya Ovchinnikova/Getty
Should researchers be paid to review papers? The question has long been fiercely debated. Now, a study has uncovered an apparent market for peer review, operating through third-party firms that offer services to scholarly publishers. 
In a preprint posted on MetaArXiv on 8 September, a team of researchers identified some 1,000 online advertisements for ‘freelance’ peer-reviewer jobs and report how they interviewed five working and retired researchers in India, Spain and the United Kingdom who say they have taken up such gigs1.
Reviewing manuscripts for journals is often considered a part of academic work, and done on a voluntary basis. But with a massive surge in manuscript submissions in recent years, it is becoming increasingly taxing. Journal editors are struggling to find willing reviewers, and the process often drags.
How to speed-up peer review: make applicants mark one another
How to speed-up peer review: make applicants mark one another
For some journals, a financial incentive is “a way to accelerate peer review so authors aren’t waiting weeks or months for a decision”, says Daniel Gorelick, editor-in-chief of Biology Open, which now pays peer reviewers for solid reports delivered within 4 business days. But “it has to be done in a transparent [and] accountable way"", and the quality of the review must always be the number one priority, he adds.
The MetaArXiv study, which hasn't yet been peer reviewed, suggests that some journals are outsourcing their peer-review process to third-party firms that hire researchers to review manuscripts, but does not provide conclusive evidence about which journals are doing so or the extent to which this is happening. Amna Pottarath, a social scientist at Radboud University in Nijmegen, the Netherlands, who led the analysis, says that these third-party firms advertise publicly but don’t list on their websites which journals are using their services. The study does not disclose the names of the firms.
Some publishers outsource operations such as copy editing, printing, doing integrity checks or managing editorial workflows, sometimes without explicit disclosure. But if journals are using third-party firms to do peer review and not disclosing them, then this “lack of transparency is concerning”, says Pottarath.
The team manually scraped advertisements from LinkedIn, company websites and other job recruitment platforms. They found that the requirements listed for the applicants included having more than eight publications in a relevant area of research in the past 10 years and previous experience in peer review. Some of the ads also mentioned that applicants must disclose their full name and institutional affiliations to the publishers.
The five researchers that Pottarath interviewed described signing up with third-party platforms to be allocated manuscripts. They say that their recruitment included background checks to verify their scientific expertise before they could sign up to review manuscripts in their field, on a first-come, first-served basis.
They then often had 2–3 days — and in some cases just 24 hours — to assess a manuscript and submit their report to the platform. Most interviewees reported receiving payments of €30–40 (around US$35–45) per review.
In the case of one company, payment was tied to the quality of the review, rated by the editors of the journal receiving it, and to speed. The study found that this company’s website listed more than 10,000 registered freelance peer reviewers, including 5,669 ‘active’ ones who were marked as having reviewed up to 50 manuscripts each.

doi: https://doi.org/10.1038/d41586-026-02277-y
Pottarath, A., Halffman, W. & Horbach, S. Preprint at MetaArXiv https://doi.org/10.31222/osf.io/2wq3b_v1 (2026).

I've been whinging about wanting to move away from Firefox for nearly a year now. It started with Mozilla's announcement about their AI strategy and my initial thoughts. Then they doubled-down and it raised my heckles even more. Then I had a long old whinge about how fucked they seem to be.
I took Vivaldi for a spin, but I couldn't get used to it. It just tries to do too much, and has way too much going on with the UI, and dark/light theme switching still doesn't work despite it apparently being fixed. So I made my peace with Firefox and stuck with it.
I kept Vivaldi on my machine as a backup, and over the last 6 months or so, I've found myself having to open it more and more due to something not working in Firefox. This was far from a daily occurrence, but it happened often enough for it to become annoying. But I still couldn't use Vivaldi full time.
It's worth stating that things not working isn't the fault of Firefox. It's developers not testing their web apps with Firefox, since it has such a small market share.
I decided to install Brave a couple months ago just to see how things went. I had some messing around to do up front, like hiding all the crypto and AI nonsense, but once that was gone, it's been fine.
Everything works how I expect, both on Ubuntu and Android. The UI is similar to Firefox, and most importantly, it doesn't try to boil the ocean. I renders webpages, then gets out of my way. I don't need it to check RSS feeds, or emails, or have toolbars on every edge of the screen. Even dark mode switching works!
I haven't felt the need to open Firefox since starting this exeperiment, so I think Brave is gonna stick.
Yes yes yes. Brave was founded by an absolute scumbag. But there's scumbags everywhere, in all walks of life. Contrary to what some people on the internet will say, my use of Brave doesn't mean I agree with, or support, Bendan Eich's world views. I consider his personal views to be separate from Brave.
You may disagree, and that's fine. But Brave is objectively a good piece of software that does what I need it to do, and does it well. So I'm gonna continue using it for the forseable future. If Firefox get their act together and become relevant again, I may jump back, but for now it will remain my secondary browser.
I do still have Vivaldi installed too. So I will continue to check in on it now and then, and if I decide they're on a par with Brave on Linux (mainly light/dark theme switching is what's missing), then I'll definitely make the switch as I feel their moral compass is far more aligned with my own.

62,2026-09-28,"For a while now, Saudi Arabia has been seeking to pivot away from fossil fuels as their main source of revenue. Even so, I did not see this coming. Ceer is a new Saudi Arabian auto manufacturer, the country's first. And they're going to produce vehicles that don't even run on the petroleum the company is known for producing.
Their inaugural model is the Exobot, an EV that will come in both sedan and SUV flavors. Giorgetto Giugiaro would've loved these wedges:

 
These are high-performance (850 horsepower) vehicles with gigantic windshields, another thing I wouldn't have expected from a country with punishing sunlight. The vehicles' air conditioning units are said to be particularly robust, having been designed specifically for the Saudi Arabian climate; the company says they can get the cabin temperature down from 149°F to 90°F (65°C to 32°C) in 10 minutes. (How crazy is it that 90°F is the target.) The idea is that you'd start the air conditioning via app, before you get into the scorching car.


Don't call those ""gullwing"" doors; Ceer calls them Shahin Wings. (Shahin is Arabic for ""falcon."")
 
 
The vehicle's ranges are respectable, with the sedan providing up to 670 km (416 miles) and the SUV up to 560 km (348 miles).
 
The dashboards are freaking curved 48"" screens that run the full width of the cabin.

 
The company has not announced prices, but says the vehicles are going into production in January. Their initial target market will be the Gulf states, with international markets to be decided on later.

63,2026-09-28,"Happy Monday! It's September 28, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.
In this morning's edition, we're looking at the EU pushing back its Tesla FSD vote, as well as a new golden era for minivans. We'll also look at Chinese car sales in Europe, and analyst predictions for Ford and GM. 
If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.
Tesla has been desperately trying to get its FSD software approved in Europe, since CEO Elon Musk declared that cars were boring and the future of the company was in robotics and autonomy — putting the car company in a bit of a tough spot in markets where its autonomy still isn't approved. Tesla had hoped to get FSD approved in Europe next month, but now it's looking like things could take until the end of the year. From Automotive News: 
STOCKHOLM — An expected European Union vote on allowing Tesla's supervised Full Self-Driving system across the bloc has been delayed from October, with the next apparent opportunity for a decision not before December, according to an EU agenda.
The EU's Technical Committee on Motor Vehicles (TCMV), which brings together representatives of EU member states and the European Commission, is due to discuss the request at its Oct. 6 meeting. The agenda lists a ""continuation of discussions"" on a Dutch request for an Article 39 authorization, with 25 minutes allocated to the discussion, rather than a vote.
Tesla's data around FSD in Europe has been wonky, so it's possible that this change from a vote to a discussion is meant to allow more time for more reliable data to come in. Or, it's possible this is entirely a scheduling thing and we shouldn't read too much into it. 
Minivans are back, baby! Sales have been climbing year after year, but now the economy and the high cost of fuel are pushing Americans out of our hulking seven-seat SUVs — and into the plush practicality of the minivan. From Bloomberg:
The much-maligned minivan is having a renaissance, as prices for both gas and swanky SUVs are near historic highs. For new parents in their 20s and 30s, who were shuttled to school at the height of the sport utility boom, vans are novel. They carry little of the suburban stigma that tanked sales after their 2000 peak. Meanwhile, empty nesters are embracing van life for its affordable practicality. Minivans offer roughly the same utility and amenities as an SUV at about three-fifths the price: They're about $31,400 cheaper on average.
I'm personally pro-vans, having road tripped in them on multiple occasions and loved it each time. They're practical, they're comfortable, they offer all sorts of interior amenities. What scares me, though, is the other reason I like vans: I can sleep in them. Are we in such a precarious economic spot that people are basing their vehicle purchases on the possibility of homelessness? 
Chinese cars are pouring into Europe, and customers are loving it. UBS, an investment bank, is less into the whole idea — probably because it doesn't stand to make any money on the new sales. For customers, though, it seems things will get better before they get worse. From Automotive News: 
Chinese vehicle exports to Europe are running at nearly double the rate of registrations, with logistics bottlenecks masking the true scale of shipments, investment bank UBS said.
The influx will likely translate into higher registrations later this year, increasing pressure on European policymakers to address the cost gap between imported Chinese electrified cars and vehicles built in Europe.
UBS estimates the current pace would translate to about 20 percent annual market share for Chinese brands, compared with nearly 10 percent through August.
""We think there is still a lack of urgency on the EU side,"" UBS analyst Patrick Hummel said during a media briefing on Sept. 25. ""The issue is more pressing than many people involved in the policymaking process understand.""
""The issue"" is ""pressing"" — the ""issue"" being affordable cars that customers want to buy. Yeah, big problem, I can see why you'd want to nip that in the bud as fast as possible. 
The war on Iran has hiked the cost of gas, and that's hitting automakers without a broad slate of EVs and hybrids hardest — automakers like GM and Ford, which analysts are saying will be this year's big losers in the automotive realm. From Reuters: 
Sept 24 (Reuters) – General Motors and Ford Motor have lost more market share in the United States than any other automakers ‌so far this year as buyers prioritize fuel efficiency amid high gas prices, an industry forecast shows.
Ford's vehicle sales are expected to drop 8.8% through the first three quarters of the year, according to a forecast released Thursday from Cox Automotive. That would knock Ford's market share down ​nearly a full percentage point, to 12.5%.
GM's US vehicle sales pace also is trailing the broader industry, expected to ​decline 6.2% year-to-date through September 30, Cox projects. The firm estimates GM will finish the quarter ⁠with a 16.7% market share, down from 17.4% a year earlier.
And this is all before Chinese automakers start building cars in the States with the full blessing of President Trump. Somehow I can't imagine that'll help things for the U.S. stalwarts.

            John Yeley has spent the last 26 years working on his farm, which his family has owned since 1852.
    

            Yeley is the seventh generation to run his farm in Illinois, just over the state line from Indiana. Although he plans to keep doing so, he’s not sure he wants his 16-year-old son and 12-year-old daughter to take over because the current economics are so rough.
    

            “Right now, the way all of this is now, no, I wouldn’t wish it on them,” he said about farming.
    

            Farmers like Yeley as well as agriculture experts agree that the farming sector is in a painful recession. Farms are dealing with record-high diesel prices, a fuel that is critical to their equipment. Trade disputes have cut off access to vital markets. Wars in Ukraine and the Middle East have sent fertilizer costs soaring, just as farmers pay higher interest rates on the loans needed to get from planting to harvest season.
    

            Yeley is not sure he’ll earn a profit this season, even with prices up for for his corn and soybeans.
    

            “We’re not a big county, but we’ve had three farmers go under this year,” said Yeley. “I know that’s on the minds of everybody, is it really worth it to keep going on? The stress level is pretty high.”
    

Diesel costs more than $6 a gallon for the first time. Here’s how that affects you


            Currently, the price of diesel is the most pressing problem for farmers.
    

            Diesel is a workhouse fuel that powers farming equipment, like tractors and combines. The national average for diesel has accelerated faster than the price of gasoline and is now near its recent all-time high of $6.53. In many states in the Midwest farm belt, the cost is even greater: The average price in Illinois, for example, was $6.82 as of Friday.
    

            Farmers generally buy thousands of gallons each year. Yeley usually buys 8,500 gallons twice a year to run his equipment.
    

            “This is the first year in my adult life that we have not bought an entire transport load either spring or fall due to fuel cost. We’ve been buying it piecemeal … 1,000 or 1,500 gallons at a time, hoping that we’d see a price drop,” he said. “Unfortunately, it’s not happening.”
    

Are you struggling with high diesel and gas prices? Share your story


            Sammy Tookey grows all manner of produce — tomatoes, corn, melons, peppers, squash, lettuce and chiles — on 10 acres in northern California’s wine country. He sells about half his harvest to a food co-op and the other half at three farmers’ markets each week.
    

            He’s feeling the squeeze from higher prices for diesel, as well as cardboard boxes and fertilizer – costs of which have also increased by double digits over the past year.
    

            Russia’s war with Ukraine first cut off supply of some of the raw materials needed to make fertilizer, boosting prices. That only got worse this year, when the war in the Middle East cut off fertilizer exports. Fertilizer prices are as much as 50% higher in the last year, according to experts and farmers.
    

            Tookey has raised his prices but not enough to keep up with ballooning expenses. He said he doesn’t want to scare off customers.
    

Trump has a ‘sledgehammer’ way to slash diesel prices. But it could boomerang back on the US


            While he plans to plant again in the spring, he’s uncertain how long he’ll remain a farmer. Tookey is getting married in late October and worries about how he’ll support children.
    

            “Being able to have a family and a farm seems almost impossible,” he said.
    

            Tookey’s stepfather, George Kibby, has a bleaker view of the current state of farming. He retired in July after 40 years in the industry because the land he leased in Orange County, California, was sold for housing.
    

            The cost of land, equipment, labor — along with the instability of prices for essentials, such as diesel and fertilizer — makes it impossible to earn a living these days, he said.
    

            “Us little farmers don’t have much of a chance,” said Kibby, who moved north with his wife this summer to help Tookey. “You work so hard not making money.”
    

            Besides the cost of raw materials, there are many other factors squeezing farmers.
    

            Most farmers take out short-term loans to cover costs between planting and harvest. Interest rates are higher as central banks raise rates to tamp down inflation, pushing up borrowing costs.
    

            Various US trade wars, particularly with China, have cut off critical markets for products like soybeans. That’s driving down prices in the short term, and farmers worry that market share will be lost in the long term.
    

            “Our farm and the vast majority of the farms (I know) are anticipating they’re going to be losing money this year, and the vast majority lost money last year,” said Aaron Lehman, president of the Iowa Farmers Union and a fifth-generation farmer.
    

‘Crisis even bigger than Covid’: American business owners are barely getting by


            “Farming does have ups and downs naturally, but when it happens year after year after year, eventually you’re no longer a good credit risk, and you can’t borrow money to keep farming,” added Lehman, who grows corn, soybeans, oats and hay.
    

            The number of farm bankruptcies rose 20% in the 12 months ending in June compared to a year ago, according to federal bankruptcy filing data. But the worst is yet to come, said Tom Ajamie, an attorney active in the farming sector.
    

            “A lot of these farmers are on the edge,” he said. “So yeah, It’s a 20% increase right now, but what happens in the next two, four, five months? A lot of farmers are just barely clinging on right now. You will see that number increase, I guarantee it.”
    

            Some congressional members from farm states, like Republican Sen. Charles Grassley of Iowa, have pressed the Trump administration to ban US diesel exports as a way to get immediate relief. But many energy experts doubt that will lower diesel prices.
    

            The American Farm Bureau Federation and other agriculture groups seek the passage of a new farm bill, which has cleared the House. The proposed legislation includes help for farmers, like raising federal farm loan limits that haven’t changed since 2018.
    

            “You get into the lame duck session, and I think we can find a bipartisan path forward on a lot of things,” said John Newton, vice president of public policy and economic analysis for the American Farm Bureau Federation, referring to the period after midterms and before the new Congress begins.
    

            Even if a bill eventually passes, even if diesel comes down later this year, that won’t help the farmers harvesting crops right now.
    

            Many told CNN that they feel like victims of things set in motion around the globe over which they have no control.
    

            “Farmers are used to dealing with unpredictability with weather, you know, soil conditions, livestock,” Lehman said. “Policy decisions that have put farmers at the right in the front of economic damage - that’s what’s very frustrating.”

65,2026-09-28,"ULTRALOQ has launched a new collection of smart locks designed to move home entry past the era of physical keys and app-dependent unlocking. The lineup, from parent company U-tec’s ULTRALOQ brand (one of the best-selling smart lock brands on the market), includes three models: the Bolt Mission, Bolt Pro, and Latch 5 Pro. Each takes a different approach to keyless access, and together they cover a range of use cases and price points.
The collection leans on two emerging technologies: ultra-wideband (UWB) for hands-free, intent-aware entry, and Aliro, an open mobile-access standard developed by the Connectivity Standards Alliance with backing from Apple, Google, and Samsung. Aliro brings the same tap-to-unlock experience consumers already use for contactless payments and digital car keys to the front door. 
All three locks are Matter Certified and compatible with Apple Home and Google Home. The Bolt Pro and Latch 5 Pro also work with Samsung SmartThings, enabling automations like turning on lights or adjusting the thermostat when the door unlocks.
The Bolt Mission ($299.99) is positioned as the first UWB-enabled smart lock on the market. It combines Bluetooth Low Energy, UWB ranging, radar-based presence sensing, and local decision logic to distinguish an authorized user approaching from outside versus someone already moving around inside the home. The practical result: the door unlocks as a user walks up, phone in pocket, without any interaction required.
Intent detection is handled locally between the phone and the lock, which keeps response times fast and avoids the need for a separate hub. The Xthings Home app simply runs in the background.
The Bolt Pro ($269.99) is the collection’s premium deadbolt. It supports Aliro-Certified digital wallet access, meaning users can unlock the door with a tap of a compatible phone or smartwatch, no app required. Digital keys live directly in a compatible mobile wallet, making the experience feel familiar to anyone who already uses contactless payments.
The Bolt Pro also supports fingerprint recognition, PIN codes, app control, and a physical key backup, covering the full range of household access needs. Fingerprint works well for everyday users, while PIN codes offer a practical option for guests, kids, or service providers.
The Latch 5 Pro ($249.99) brings the same Aliro-Certified, tap-to-unlock experience to lever-style doors, making it a natural fit for side entrances, home offices, garage-entry doors, rentals, and shared spaces. It supports fingerprint access and PIN codes for temporary users, and carries the same Matter Certification and smart home platform compatibility as the rest of the lineup.
“The front door is moving away from keys and apps toward digital credentials and intent-aware access,” said Raj Sundar, Senior Director of Product Management for ULTRALOQ. “Our goal with this collection is to make that transition seamless and practical for customers.”
All three models are available now through ULTRALOQ’s website and Amazon, with availability through The Home Depot online coming in October. More details can be found at www.ultraloq.com.

67,2026-09-28,"Nearly half a million people are displaced in Rakhine as a result of the conflict between the military and rebel groups.
Save
Share
An air strike carried out by Myanmar’s military has killed at least 33 people in territory controlled by rebel factions.
The attack near a market in the north of the Rakhine state in the west of the country on Monday also wounded 36 people, rescue and rebel group officials reported. The strikes are the latest in a series of assaults against pro-democracy and ethnic armed groups by the military government, which took power in a coup in 2021, sparking armed opposition.
The Arakan army said a fighter jet dropped two bombs near a local market in the town of Kyauktaw at noon local time (0530 GMT), killing 33 people, including vendors, shoppers, and pedestrians. It said the death toll “may continue to increase”.
Wai Hun Aung, a senior rescue official in Rakhine, confirmed the details to the Associated Press news agency. He said the market was the region’s main wholesale centre, drawing traders and shoppers from across the state.
“This is one of the most severe airstrikes in Rakhine,” he said. Photos and videos posted on social media showed buildings on fire, damaged vehicles and parts of a nearby bridge destroyed.
Myanmar’s military did not immediately comment, but it has previously said it strikes only legitimate military targets and accused resistance groups of “terrorism”.
Myanmar has been gripped by ⁠conflict since the elected ⁠government led by Aung San Suu Kyi was ousted in the 2021 coup. A nationwide resistance movement has since evolved into a civil war between the ‌military government and an array of armed ethnic and pro-democracy groups.
President Min Aung Hlaing, Myanmar’s former military government chief, was elected by ‌a military-backed Parliament in April, formalising his grip on power after five years of army rule.
The Arakan army is a well-trained and well-armed military group seeking autonomy for Rakhine’s ethnic minority. It launched an offensive in November 2023 and has since captured strategically important military headquarters and 14 of the state’s 17 townships.
The military has imposed severe restrictions on trade routes to Rakhine since the fighting there intensified, worsening food shortages.
In December, a military airstrike on a hospital in Rakhine’s Mrauk U township killed at least 33 people and wounded 70 others.
Nearly half a million people are displaced in Rakhine as a result of the conflict, according to United Nations data. The UN added in August that the human rights situation in Myanmar has plummeted “to a new low”, citing “brutal abuse” against the Rohingya minority and the devastating impact of illicit economic activity, including illegal mining for rare earth minerals.
Rebel groups and Myanmar’s military are now both using drones in the protracted conflict, which has killed more than 100,000 people, according to the Armed Conflict Location and Event Data (ACLED) project.

Back in July 2026, OnePlus officially discontinued product rollouts in the United States. That means new OnePlus phones won't be directly available to American consumers going forward. But on top of being a popular phone brand that many consider is better than Samsung Galaxy for phone gaming, OnePlus also makes highly rated earbuds, smartwatches, and tablets — and those won't be coming stateside anymore, either.
There are several OnePlus phones that have software support until 2029 among those that were sold in the U.S. before the company exited the market. Likewise, any other OnePlus products you bought before the company pulled out of North America may still be supported for a bit longer. Here's what to expect for the latest OnePlus accessories:
For tablets, the OnePlus Pad Lite loses security update support on July 8, 2031, and the OnePlus Pad Go 2 loses support on December 24, 2031. The OnePlus Watch 3 has until March 2027, the OnePlus Watch 3 43 mm is supported until July 2027, and the OnePlus Watch Lite loses support starting in November 2027. The latest earbuds, the OnePlus Buds 4, ended security update support back in July 2026. However, the company assures its North American customers that first-party repair services are still available for these products via the official OnePlus support page.
Reviewers have praised official OnePlus accessories for their customizability, performance, and attractive minimalism. They are also known for being part of an unusual mid-range pricing model — which actually might explain what happened to OnePlus to cause the company to fall out of the U.S. market in the end. But if you're looking for high-quality Android accessories to replace your OnePlus favorites, you don't necessarily have to resort to mainstream options like the Google Pixel Watch or Samsung Galaxy Buds.
One popular consensus is that OnePlus users should switch to Nothing or Motorola. The Nothing Headphone (a) and CMF Watch 3 Pro are well-reviewed accessories that emphasize value and utility, and the Nothing brand itself was co-founded by OnePlus co-founder Carl Pei, so there's a shared DNA. Motorola makes the Moto Buds 2 Plus, the Moto Watch, and the Moto Pad (available only through the Motorola online store or certain wireless carriers). We reviewed the Moto Buds 2 Plus and were impressed by Motorola's ability to manufacture such well-priced accessories with features that compete strongly against the more premium alternatives."
