Last updatedUpdated: 
Sep 28, 2026


by Simon SpÃ¤ti Â· CreatedCreated: Sep 26, 2026 Â· 4 min read

recently updated

Recent changes

Sep 28today
published Â· 882 words




If we think Is writing code dead, and AI is generating all codebases, I still think the bigger problem is people or full teams not knowing anything anymore about the system architecture or the intent behind why certain choices have been made.
A comment on a discussion I had:
I think AI writes probably average code (depending on the task and size). So if your code base was below average AI can easily improve it up to average. At least that’s what I’ve observed here.
To me, the problem is not the AI code, but that nobody knows anything, and everyone just asks Claude. You end up with no plan whatsoever.

This tweet summarizes the current state at fast moving startups well, or larger companies or where middle management is pushing AI hard:
I am done with this shit. It is over. The state of engineering right now is horrible. It has been half a month since I started a new role at a big company. Nobody knows anything here. The specs, code, tests, PRDs, tickets, resolution of those tickets, reports, etc., everything is made by Claude Code.
Nobody on my team likes this. They are being forced to ship as much as they can. I have heard multiple times from higher management that pushing code is not a bottleneck, so why are we slow? People are working 12 to 13 hours a day just to press enter. Nobody is reading anything. Humans in corporate are doing nothing on their own.
Everyone, literally everyone, from an L1 to an L7 engineer here is doing the same thing. Talk to Claude. There is no sense of victory. Nobody is resolving bugs. In reality, nobody is thinking anymore. Everything is done by LLMs.
It is so soul-sucking. I would not mind it, to be honest, if we were at least given the time to check out the code and see what is going where. But no, the goal is to just ship. No matter what happens. 

Voxium
Hoyt Emerson mention that data engineering is different:
I think Data people are different. Weâve had to know everything about the product/business from day 1. AI just removes friction for us now. 

Tweet
I think data people who grew up pre-AI had to know everything (or a lot, or involve domain experts) to figure it out, indeed. But AI makes this obsolete, or seemingly obsolete.
That’s why people starting today, or me as well, if I start today prompting away in a new field, all of a sudden, that knowledge is missing.
Good point by 

Sean Behan:
Iâve always admired product people who canât code but can manage a team to get the software they want. Knowing what you want has always been the hardest part.
One could say a good product manager could now build anything they want and find a market, make it look good, etc. But then again, if you can’t code, you will essentially build a very bad foundation for a product that’s very hard to maintain (although AI is getting better at that too, especially when you iterate often, but still, if you choose the wrong language or the wrong mental model, you have the wrong start from the get-go).
It still helps to know the fundamentals, either way: for programming and designing a product, and for a good PM who knows what is needed but also understands system and architecture design.
Thinking in systems, or architectures, or having intent and design- all of them help to be a better software engineer. Nowadays, Writing code by hand might be dead, but it certainly helps, and Having Taste (with AI) is more important than ever.
But the final boss is, and always will be, maintainability. The easier it is to generate a quick pipeline, app, or BI dashboard, the more you have to maintain. And if nobody knows a thing, that can get really hard.
Yes, the AI can’t prompt itself, right? Why 

do we even need humans? To me, it’s a clear sign that humans are still needed to direct and orchestrate it. That’s also why intent, taste, design, and architecture are all killer features in today’s world.
But once these are absent, or even worse, fundamental, get lost, it’s really dangerous. I read today that this is a self-inflicted problem, and if we still hired juniors, then the problem wouldn’t be happening. But yeah, it’s not as easy.
Origin: the primagen video and Limitations of LLMs and AI
References: What I Learned Writing with AI
This note is part of my Second Brain, a 650+ public notes on data engineering, life, and more. I send a digest of new notes, blog posts, and book chapters, and general updates by email. Subscribe below if you'd like to get the next one."
84,2026-09-28,"
In Cars, iCaur, International News				 / by Mick Chan				 / 28 September 2026 6:23 pm


The iCaur V27 Long Range Edition will launch in China at the end of this month on September 30, bringing a variant of the V27 range-extended EV SUV with even longer EV range, reported Car News China.
Equipped with a 51.2 kWh battery pack from CATL, this is a 49.3% increase in battery capacity over the 34.3 kWh battery version which was one of two variants previewed for the Thailand market last month, and this Long Range Edition offers a claimed 300 km of EV range on the CLTC cycle, or around 246 km on the WLTP standard. 
The report cites Chery as stating that the Long Range Edition supports DC charging that will bring a 30-80% recharge in 17 minutes, and its V2L (vehicle-to-load) capability is increased from 3.3 kW to 6.0 kW. 
            








Powertrain for the Long Range Edition that is to launch in China is unchanged from the 34.3 kWh version, which is a dual-motor AWD configuration that outputs 455 PS and 505 Nm combined. The range extender part of the ensemble is a 1.5 litre Atkinson-cycle four-cylinder turbocharged petrol engine that makes 155 PS and 220 Nm. 
The exterior dimensions of the V27 Long Range Edition are unchanged from those of its stablemates, measuring 4,909 mm long (5,055 mm including the tailgate-mounted spare wheel), 1,976 mm wide and 1,894 mm tall with a 2,910 mm wheelbase. 
In terms of off-road-centric figures, approach and departure angles are 24 degrees and 23 degrees respectively, water wading depth is rated at 600 mm, and the body has a torsional rigidity of 31,186 Nm per degree. 
                        iCaur V27 4WD in Thailand
In Thailand, the V27 4WD with the 34.3 kWh battery (150 km NEDC, or around 130 km WLTP) is accompanied by a 2WD powertrain version that gets a 20.4 kWh battery pack rated to give 95 km (NEDC) of EV range, or around 80 km on the WLTP standard. 
            






While it may wear overt off-roader styling, the V27 is of a unibody construction that is the staple of road-biased SUVs, and suspension is by MacPherson struts in front and a multi-link suspension configuration at the rear. Rolling stock on the dual-motor 4WD version is a set of 21-inch aero-style wheels. 
Inside, the V27 gets a slim 8.8-inch instrument display and a 15.4-inch infotainment touchscreen on a Qualcomm Snapdragon 8295 processor, and there are toggle switches on the roof including auxiliary switches for wiring up accessories. Dual smartphone holders include one wireless charger, while the second row gets air-con vents and mounting points on the backs of the front seats for attachments such as a tablet holder.
                                                iCaur V27 4WD in Thailand
The iCaur V27 is claimed to offer 960 mm of rear seat knee room, while 50 compartments area included throughout the cabin. Its luggage compartment holds 715 litres, increasing to 1,818 litres with the rear seats folded. 
Other equipment in the Thai-spec V27 includes dual-zone auto air-con, six-way power-adjustable front seats with ventilation and driver’s side memory, cooled front armrest storage, a 6 kW vehicle-to-load (V2L) function, a 15-speaker Pioneer sound system and a 360-degree camera system with transparency. Seven airbags (including a centre airbag) and Level 2 driving assistance also features. 
The iCaur V27 has been sighted in Malaysia, too; iCaur Malaysia has previously confirmed that the V27 will be sold in Malaysia, and its launch could coincide with the start of CKD local assembly for this model. 
            






GALLERY: iCaur V27 REEV in Thailand (images by Autolife Thailand)

The iCaur V27 is claimed to offer 960 mm of rear seat knee room, while 50 compartments area included throughout the cabin. Its luggage compartment holds 715 litres, increasing to 1,818 litres with the rear seats folded. 
Other equipment in the Thai-spec V27 includes dual-zone auto air-con, six-way power-adjustable front seats with ventilation and driver’s side memory, cooled front armrest storage, a 6 kW vehicle-to-load (V2L) function, a 15-speaker Pioneer sound system and a 360-degree camera system with transparency. Seven airbags (including a centre airbag) and Level 2 driving assistance also features.

      Commentary
  



      Commentary
  

How quickly displaced workers find another job, and what happens to their earnings afterwards, may offer a fuller picture of Singapore’s labour market adjustment, says NTU’s Chua Yeow Hwee.
Office workers in the central business district of Singapore. (File photo: iStock)

       This audio is generated by an AI tool.
    
SINGAPORE: 4,620 retrenchments. The number jumps out from Singapore's labour market figures for the second quarter of 2026, marking the highest quarterly total since the fourth quarter of 2020.
But the number of retrenchments is only one measure of how Singapore's labour market is performing.
Singapore’s labour market remains relatively healthy. Employment continued to grow, unemployment remained low, and there were still 1.48 job vacancies for every unemployed person.
What deserves closer attention is what happens after someone loses a job. How quickly do they return to work? What kind of job do they eventually find?


CNA Games






Guess Word

      Show More
  




      Show Less
  



For this, we look at the rate of re-entry into employment. Among retrenched residents, the proportion who returned to employment within six months was 54.9 per cent in the second quarter, down from 60.7 per cent in the first quarter.
These figures should be interpreted carefully because they refer to different groups of workers. The latest figure covers residents retrenched in the fourth quarter of 2025, while the previous figure covers those retrenched in the third quarter of 2025.
Following the same group over a longer period offers another perspective. Among residents retrenched in the second quarter of 2025, 57.4 per cent had returned to employment within six months, rising to 69.8 per cent after a year.
The figures show that more displaced workers find employment over a longer period. But why might some workers take longer to secure a new job when Singapore continues to add jobs?
One possible explanation lies in uneven employment opportunities across sectors. 
Though total employment grew by 11,400 in the second quarter, most of the increase came from non-residents, mainly Work Permit holders in construction and manufacturing. These jobs may offer limited opportunities for retrenched professionals with different skills and experience.
Resident employment grew by a modest 2,200, mostly in domestic-oriented sectors. Information and communications and financial services also added some resident workers.
However, hiring in these sectors occurred alongside retrenchments. Retrenchments were concentrated in manufacturing, information and communications, and financial services. Job vacancies also fell from 73,300 in March to 68,600 in June, driven mainly by fewer PMET openings in information and communications and financial services.
These patterns show that hiring and retrenchments can occur within the same sector, even as job vacancies decline. The opportunities available may differ from the types of jobs that retrenched workers previously held, making it harder for some to find suitable employment.
Finding a suitable match can thus take time, even when jobs are available. Some workers face a trade-off between returning to work quickly and holding out for a role closer to their previous responsibilities, seniority or pay.
The speed of re-employment captures only part of a worker’s transition. It leaves another part of the adjustment unseen: the kind of job that worker returns to.
For experienced workers, part of the difficulty may be carrying accumulated experience into a new setting.
A mid-career professional may have spent 15 years building knowledge, relationships and expertise. Some of that experience transfers readily across firms and industries. Other skills may command less value outside the worker’s previous occupation or sector.
Consider, for illustration, a professional earning S$8,400 a month, the 2025 median for full-time employed resident PMEs, excluding employer CPF contributions. Suppose the worker is retrenched and, six months later, finds a job paying S$6,000, roughly what a PME at the 25th percentile earns.
Employment statistics would record a successful re-entry. However, the worker has gone six months without a salary, potentially offset by retrenchment benefits or other financial support, and now faces substantially lower monthly earnings.
Re-employment, therefore, tells us whether someone found another job, but less about how much of the economic loss from retrenchment has been recovered.
The support workers need depends on the challenges they face. Some may need help identifying suitable vacancies, while others require additional training to move into new roles or sectors.
Singapore already has a broad set of measures to support workers through career transitions, including career coaching, job matching, Career Conversion Programmes and reskilling support.
Career Conversion Programmes in particular can bridge training and job matching by training workers for actual roles that companies need, “instead of learning a new skill and then hoping to find a job that uses it,” as Acting Manpower Minister Jasmin Lau put it.
Employers can also contribute by recognising how skills and experience gained in one sector may apply in another.
One quarter of weaker six-month re-entry is insufficient to establish a persistent deterioration in Singapore’s labour market.
Some degree of job turnover is a normal feature of a dynamic economy. Firms expand, contract and reorganise as economic conditions change. 
The concern is how costly the transition becomes for affected workers, particularly when finding a new job involves a substantial loss of earnings.
The next few quarters will help establish whether the recent decline in six-month re-entry is temporary or whether some workers are taking longer to find employment. 
Equally important is what happens to their earnings after they return to work. Both measures are needed to understand how successfully Singapore's labour market is adjusting.
Chua Yeow Hwee is an Assistant Professor in Economics at the Nanyang Technological University (NTU). He is also the Deputy Director of the Economic Growth Centre at NTU. The opinions expressed are those of the writer and do not represent the views and opinions of the institutions that he is in.

Gears of War: E-Day has a monstrous 115GB install requirement on Xbox, so I've rounded the best options to improve your Xbox’s storage size to accommodate it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Get ready, players, because Gears of War: E-Day is nearly here, and it’s shaping up to be one of the best games in the Gears of War franchise and the biggest gaming blockbuster of the year, second only to Grand Theft Auto VI.
However, there is one caveat Xbox players must be aware of before buying it on October 6 (or October 1, if they've secured early access via pre-ordering the game), and that is its required install space of 115GB (as confirmed on its Steam page).
That’s about one-tenth of a standard Xbox Series X’s 1TB storage space, and that’s just for the base game alone without any DLC. Plus, the developers plan (confirmed by GameSpot) to update Gears of War: E-Day’s Horde Mode with new classes, missions, modifiers, and seasonal content, along with adding other in-game items like gun cosmetics and character skins, so that 115GB install requirement is going to bloat fast.
So, we have sought out the best Xbox Series X|S storage expansion devices, from external SSDs to plug-and-play SSD cards, to help you fit in Gears of War: E-Day while having tons to spare for its post-launch updates and other awesome Xbox Series X|S titles for your Xbox.
Now, granted, finding Xbox external storage upgrades is a lot harder these days thanks to the ongoing RAM crisis, jacking up the prices of all things tech-related while making their availability scarcer.
One of the more affordable and available options is external hard drives, which can offer tons of storage space ranging from 512GB, 1TB, 2TB, and even 4TB. In addition, they can be formatted to store all kinds of media, not just Xbox games, depending on what platform you intend to use it for.
The drawback is that you can’t play Xbox games directly from an external hard drive, you have to manually transfer them from the hard drive onto your Xbox’s internal hard drive if you wish to play them.
“The Samsung T7 portable SSD is every bit as good as the T7 Touch but at a lower price. It makes it a brilliant all-purpose portable SSD that can be used for both computers and the latest Xbox consoles.” ~ Richard Devine, Managing Editor, Windows CentralWindows Central review: ⭐⭐⭐⭐½
“Western Digital's P40 Game Drive SSD is a stellar external storage upgrade for Xbox Series X|S consoles, and is even better on PC with RGB customization and faster speeds. If you're only getting this for Xbox, though, there may be more affordable options.” ~ Zachary Boddy, former Staff Writer, Windows CentralWindows Central review: ⭐⭐⭐⭐
“If you specifically need a super portable external SSD for playing your classic Xbox games and storing Series X|S games, then the Samsung T5 EVO is a fantastic pick. It has a slim form factor that can easily fit in your pocket or in a bag. Plus, it's drop-resistant to better withstand jostling on the go.” ~ Rebecca Spear, former gaming staff writer, Windows Central
Best Premium USB from our best Xbox SSDs in 2025 list
Plug & Play expansion cards are scarcer and more expensive, but more convenient Xbox storage expansion units compared to external hard drives.
They fit easily into an Xbox’s dedicated expansion card slot, so they don’t take up desk space, and they let players play games installed on the cards directly without needing to shuffle them into the Xbox’s internal SSD, saving you from tedious file management.
However, you can’t reformat these Plug & Play Xbox expansion cards for other devices, as they were purely designed for Xbox consoles.
The SanDisk Optimus GX C50 is the newest Xbox storage Expansion Card line on the block.It comes in multiple storage models from 512GB to 2TB, with low-latency performance that matches Xbox Velocity Architecture, so games can be played directly from it. Plus, it’s compatible with Xbox’s Quick Resume feature, so players can swap between multiple games without losing progress.Plus, it has a free one-month trial membership for Xbox Game Pass Ultimate.
“Western Digital's long-awaited competitor to the exorbitant Seagate Expansion Card for Xbox Series X|S is functionally and practically great, and it has lowered prices.” ~ Zachary Boddy, former Staff Writer, Windows CentralWindows Central review: ⭐⭐⭐⭐
""Microsoft and Seagate partnered up on a slick memory card-style SSD expansion for Xbox Series X and Xbox Series S, filling demand where standard hard drives simply don't cut it.” Matt Brown, former Staff Writer, Windows CentralWindows Central review: ⭐⭐⭐⭐
If you don’t mind waiting for discounts, it may be wise to wait until these external hard drives and plug-and-play cards go on sale, as many of them have retail prices that cost hundreds of dollars as of this writing.
However, the RAM crisis is stirring extreme levels of FOMO (Fear of Missing Out) behavior in consumers, driving them to buy popular Seagate and WD_Black Xbox expansion cards online until they run out of stock, even at full price.
With this in mind, it may also be a risky but good idea to grab one of these devices quickly because they may sell out before they even get a chance to be discounted during sales events like Amazon's Big Deal Days in October or Black Friday in November.
And there you have all the best options to upgrade your Xbox’s Storage size in time for Gears of War: E-Day. Are you looking forward to playing Gears of War: E-Day? Will you be sticking around to play its Horde Siege mode months after launch to see its post-game updates?
If you have any thoughts on the matter, please send them to the comments section below, or our Discord, Reddit, or cast a vote in the poll above.
Gears of War: E-Day is scheduled to launch on Xbox Series X|S (with Xbox Play Anywhere support), Xbox Game Pass, and PC via Windows and Steam on October 1, 2026, for early-access players who pre-ordered the game, and then October 6, 2026, for everyone else.

The average rate on a 30-year mortgage is back above 7% for the first time in twenty months. It’s rough news for the housing market and homebuyers already struggling with rising prices elsewhere. It comes as Treasury yields, which impact borrowing costs across the economy, hit multi-decade highs. Amna Nawaz discussed more with David Wessell of the Hutchins Center on Fiscal and Monetary Policy.
Notice: Transcripts are machine and human generated and lightly edited for accuracy. They may contain errors.
Amna Nawaz:
The average rate on a 30-year mortgage is back above 7 percent for the first time in 20 months. It's rough news for the housing market and for homebuyers already struggling with rising prices elsewhere.
And it comes as treasury yields, which impact borrowing costs across the economy, hit multidecade highs and after inflation fears forced the Federal Reserve to hike interest rates for the first time in more than three years.
To help us understand what all of this means, we're joined again by David Wessel. He's director of the Hutchins Center on Fiscal and Monetary Policy at the Brookings Institution.
David, always good to see you.
David Wessel, Brookings Institution:
Good to be here.
Amna Nawaz:
Before we turn to the bond market, the average 30-year fixed-rate mortgage currently sits at 7.03 percent, according to Freddie Mac. What does the rate going above 7 percent mean for homebuyers and the housing market?
David Wessel:
Right.
Well, actually today, according to Bankrate, which is the daily thing, it's up to 7.2 percent today. A year ago, it was 6.3 percent. So on a mortgage of $250,000, that's an extra $1,800 a year in payments. It's like having a 13th month of mortgage payments. So it's a big deal.
And what it means is that, A, houses will be more expensive. So, many people will be priced out of the market. You need to have more income to support that. And, secondly, it means -- and we have seen this from a number of the big homebuilders, Pulte, Lennar, D.R. Horton -- they are anticipating less demand so they're going to build fewer houses, which, of course, is not what we need now at a time when we know we need more houses.
Amna Nawaz:
Let me ask you about the bond market now. It's been getting a lot of attention. Help our viewers understand, what exactly is the bond market, why does it matter, and why so much anxiety around it in recent months?
David Wessel:
Well, the -- when we talk about mortgage rates, why do mortgage rates move? They move because they're pegged to what happens in the bond market. And the bond market is a big financial market. There's $32 trillion worth of U.S. Treasury debt in the bond market.
That's about the same size as all the stocks on the New York Stock Exchange, and then, if you add in corporate borrowing or state and local governments, another $16 billion. So -- $16 trillion.
So, what this means is that people are buying and selling Treasury bonds. They're driving up the yield because there's so much more borrowing in the economy. And unlike the stock market, which matters if you own stock, the bond market yields affect our borrowing rates, not only mortgages, but it affects what you pay for an auto loan.
And it affects what the government has to pay to borrow. And the federal government in particular borrows a lot of money. So when rates go up, the Treasury has to pay more to borrow. Eventually, that widens the deficit and eventually that will mean higher taxes.
Amna Nawaz:
So, the yields on 10-year Treasury bonds, for example, hit 5.2 percent today. That's the highest we have seen since January of 2002.
David Wessel:
Right.
Amna Nawaz:
You mentioned borrowing. Is that the sole driver behind those rising rates?
David Wessel:
Well, there's a supply and demand thing.
So the supply of borrowing is how much money the Treasury borrows, and the demand is how much investors are willing to pay for it. And what's happened is, the Treasury is borrowing more and more because we're running big deficits. And now there's a new player. All these hyperscalers are borrowing a lot at long maturities, 10-year, 20-year, 30 years.
And that used to be the place where the -- where the Treasury had -- didn't have much competition. So what's happened is, we have a strong economy. People in the markets expect the Fed to keep raising interest rates, and the bond market responds to what future interest rates will be.
You have more concerns about inflation being persistent because of what's going on in the Middle East. You have these new borrowers and the hyperscalers. And on top...
Amna Nawaz:
Hyperscalers are the AI data centers too.
David Wessel:
Hyperscalers, Amazon, Meta, Google, and stuff like that.
So -- and at the same time, there's a little bit of anxiety in the market about the credibility of the U.S. government. Will we get our budget deficit under control? Will the government function? I think there's a lot more confidence in the Federal Reserve now because of what Kevin Warsh did recently raising interest rates.
But it used to be the Treasury could borrow at rates cheaper than everybody else. The economists call it the convenience yield, and that's eroded over time, which is a bad sign.
Amna Nawaz:
So, for the American consumer, big picture, they have got higher prices for gas, for groceries, health care. The cost of living is going up.
We have heard the president repeatedly boast about this being the greatest economy in history and also saying that all of these disruptions are short-term, that this pain and the higher costs are temporary. Are there any signs to back that up?
David Wessel:
Well, first of all, the president has a tendency to hyperbole, and this is a good instance. The economy has been pretty good, surprisingly resilient. Unemployment by historical standards is pretty low.
The problem has been inflation, which is why the Fed is raising interest rates. The one thing that could end -- but part of this is the president's fault. Tariffs have pushed up inflation, and the war in Iran has definitely pushed up inflation.
So you tell me what the president is going to do on tariffs, you tell me whether the president is going to find a way to end the war in Iran and bring down oil prices, you tell me whether the president and his Treasury secretary actually have a plan to reduce the federal deficit, and I will tell you when interest rates are going to go down.
Amna Nawaz:
Dare I ask you how high you think mortgage rates could potentially go? Already at 7 percent.
David Wessel:
I have no idea.
(Laughter)
David Wessel:
But, look, they have gone up quite a bit. What will happen is, this will be like a brake on the economy, B-R-A-K-E.
And if it slows the economy some, that will take some pressure off of prices and take some pressure off of the inflation. But, for now, we just have -- the economy is doing pretty well, and there's a lot of demand for money, a lot of demand for borrowing, and that's pushing it up.
Amna Nawaz:
Always appreciate you telling us everything you know and what we don't yet know.
David Wessel:
Happy to tell you what I don't know.
Amna Nawaz:
David Wessel, always great to see you. Thank you so much.
David Wessel:
You're welcome.

BTC/USD four-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Sudden, conspicuous liquidity at a price level often signals a calculated attempt by large traders to influence price direction. 


BTC liquidation heatmap. Source: CoinGlass

91,2026-09-28,"
  
As the saying goes, timing is everything. If Tesla CEO and on-again, off-again trillionaire Elon Musk intended to gin up publicity for the company’s long-awaited re-launch of the iconic Tesla Roadster, now is the perfect time. Or not, as the case may be. The affordability crisis is front and center in the 2026 election cycle, and a $200,000 electric sports car is perhaps not the best foot forward for a political influencer who has been pouring dollar after dollar into dozens of races across the country, aiming to prop up Republican candidates for office in the runup to Election Day 2026.
The saying also goes that any hit is a good hit in the public relations field, but that depends on who is reading what room. For example, last week Saudi Arabia saw fit to unveil the first two “EXOBOT” electric cars to roll off the assembly line at the brand new factory of state-backed Ceer Motors, the nation’s first in-house automaker. The launch was a signal of confidence in the future of the Kingdom, even as the fallout from US President Donald Trump’s war in Iran continues to ripple across the region.
Ceer has yet to disclose prices for the new sedan and SUV, though the sales pitch hints that a fat wallet is in order. “The mission is clear: to design, build, and offer aspirational battery electric vehicles to the vibrant communities of Saudi Arabia and the GCC,” the Saudi sovereign wealth fund PIF said in a press statement.
Readers, what do you think? How much will a Ceer set you back? About $30,000 USD is generally considered an affordable price point for an electric car in the US nowadays. At $60,000 the higher end of the affordability scale kicks in. Double that, and you’re knocking at the door of aspirational mode.
The Ceer launch is all well and good for Saudi Arabia, where the nation’s leaders don’t face the chore of turning out their voters every four years. Here in the US it’s a different story, and the story is wealth and corruption. After a 10-year run of beguiling the US electorate, Trump has lost his magic touch, largely but not exclusively due to the fallout from his war of choice against Iran. Fuel prices are shooting up alongside farm bankruptcies, and that’s just the tip of a long and ugly iceberg of economic woes burdened by an electorate burdened with the rising cost of, well, everything.
Into this picture steps Elon Musk and his soon-to-be unveiled $200,000 Tesla Roadster. Electric mobility fans can also claim a founders title if they shell out $250,000, but let’s say $200,00 for the sake of simplicity.
The original Roadster sold for a base price of around $100,000 when Tesla Motors launched it in 2006, which qualified it as an aspirational purchase in its day. Accounting for inflation, that would come to about $166,000 today.
Evidently Tesla is determined up the ante on aspirational with the new Roadster. Aside from shelling out a total of $200,000 somewhere down the line, prospective buyers need to lay down $5,000 off their credit card just to hold a place for the following 10 days, within which period they are required to pony up an additional fee of $45,000 by wire transfer. “Reservations are not final until the wire transfer payment is received,” Tesla advises.
Now, who has that kind of cash lying around?
The big reveal is reportedly scheduled for October 1, which is just around the corner. The big question is whether or not Musk will show up in person. Lately, he’s been a no-show. He skipped out on the all-important Cybercab driverless robotaxi event in Austin, Texas on September 3, and he was equally not visible when Tesla celebrated the official opening of its Semi Class 8 electric truck factory in Nevada last week.
Personally, I’m betting that Musk will show up for this one, if only to show up the folks at Ceer. Musk also has his reputation on the line. He first teased the return of the Roadster nine years ago, back in 2017 with production slated for 2020. After years of delay the hype has reached fever pitch.
In April Musk hinted that the launch event will be one of the most “exciting product unveils ever.”
“Success is not guaranteed, but excitement is,” he also said during a recent video appearance on the “All In” podcast hosted by Jason Calacanis, leading some to speculate that Musk intends to cast himself as Howard Stark in the infamous hovercar scene from the 2011 move Captain America: The First Avenger, with the new Roadster recreating the hovering of Stark’s 1942 Cadillac.
Somewhat on brand, in the movie Stark promises that the airborne car will be available “in a few short years,” only to see it hit the ground in a cascade of sparks and smoke.
Aspirational sports cars aside, the up front cost of an electric vehicle continues to drop, largely due to cost-cutting on the battery side. Ford is among the US automakers also banking that a new approach to assembly line operations will reduce costs.
Trump’s war of choice in Iran has also tipped the TCO (total cost of ownership) calculation further onto the EV side, largely on account of the increased price of fuel. The high cost of motor oil is also factoring in to a lesser degree.
A new analysis of data from the UK’s “MOT” vehicle safety test program adds even more support to the TCO case for electric vehicles. The analysis, commissioned by the trade organization British Vehicle Rental and Leasing Association, affirms that EVs are less prone to mechanical breakdown than their gas-powered counterparts, with the reliability gap increasing as the mileage adds up.
That’s good news for the many US households that don’t have access to mass transit or other alternative means of transportation. Cars are a fact of life that can make or break a person’s ability to make a living, get an education, visit their doctor, or simply go out for a night at the movies.
Speaking of movies, after the dust settles from the big Tesla Roadster reveal on October 1, audiences in New York and Los Angeles can get a sneak peek at the new documentary “Musk,” unspooling initially on October 9 with wide release following on October 16. Check out the trailer here.
Trivia fans may recognize the name of “Musk” director Alex Gibney, an Emmy- and Oscar-winning filmmaker widely known for his documentaries exploring the Enron and Theranos scandals. Gibney also attached his name to Chris Paine’s 2006 documentary, “Who Killed the Electric Car?” which tracked the sad fate of General Motors’s 1990’s-era EV-1. A lot of water has passed under the bridge since then…
Image: The long-awaited unveiling of the new $200,000 Tesla Roadster electric sports car is set for October 1, just in time to stoke the fires of the affordability crisis in the runup to Election Day 2026 (screenshot courtesy of Tesla).

92,2026-09-28,Steelmaker Stelco Holdings says it's idling some production at its Hamilton plant in response to U.S. tariffs.
93,2026-09-28,"Recently, I've found myself asking the same question over and over again: Did AI write this? If you spend any amount of time online, you probably have, too. Just a few short years since the advent of ChatGPT, the internet has devolved into a bot-generated wasteland. Experts warned us that the slopocalypse was coming, and it is here.
The world isn't taking this lying down. Publishers are canceling book contracts. Op-eds by billionaires and professors and politicians are getting outed. The uproar has become so intense that companies are scrambling to respond. LinkedIn is encouraging users to flag suspicious posts. Substack is tagging suspected AI writing with a label. Google is building tools to help people check whether the images and videos they see were made with AI. Anthropic is now watermarking the text Claude generates. That has stirred up its own backlash, mounted by people who say these efforts to identify and root out AI authorship are antiquated, punitive, and defamatory.
We're now all trying to figure out just how much of our writing we want to cede to AI. Maybe you think it should be used to write everything. Maybe you think it should be used to write absolutely nothing. Chances are, you're probably like me, drifting somewhere in between these two shores, uneasy with its ubiquity and also eager to get its help. Every single one of us has drawn and redrawn our own lines, clashing with everyone else's, making us incredibly mad in the process.
To have any hope of reaching a shared norm, we need to answer a deeper question: Why does it matter so much to us in the first place whether something originated from a human mind or an AI? We don't think twice about all kinds of things that were once made by people and are now made by machines — textiles, furniture, the box of chocolate chip cookies I couldn't resist buying at the store. So why is writing — or for that matter, drawing or composing music or creating videos — any different? Who cares, as long as it's good?
I cared. Why else was I squinting at every suspicious passage I encountered, like a detective manically swabbing for the bot's fingerprints?
So I turned to an AI detector. Study after study has shown that most people are actually bad at spotting machine-generated writing, and Pangram — the leading AI detection tool on the market — claims to be 99.7% accurate. By seeing clearly who or what wrote every single sentence I consumed for a few weeks, I thought I'd get a little closer to understanding why this distinction is so important to us.
I started my investigation with LinkedIn. I figured the mecca for the world's most shameless corporate professionals was where I was most likely to face the onslaught of AI writing head-on. With Pangram's Chrome extension, every post and comment in my newsfeed showed up with a label: a red rectangle with a robot's head marked ""AI"" (at least 80% AI-generated); a green rectangle with a person's head marked ""Human"" (at least 90% human-written); or a yellow rectangle with a robot's head marked ""Mixed."" As I scrolled through my newsfeed, I scanned for the robot's head as if I were a lion in the savannah scanning for prey.
One founder I know, Peter Pang, appeared to have not posted a single human-written thing in more than a year. Pang is a cofounder of Creao AI, an agentic AI platform, and his posts were jam-packed with AI's tells (one example: ""AI doesn't just make engineers faster. It forces you to rethink what engineering even is""). Did he even look at what his agents drafted before they blasted it to his 3,000-plus followers?
When I asked him, he said he does review and tinker with what his agent prepares for him, ""including this reply"" (Pangram flagged his email to me as 100% AI-generated). He gives the agent some ideas or an outline, after which it pulls research and drafts something for him to read. ""The final text carries my views,"" he said, ""and I am the one who hits publish.""
Other people in my network were more occasional AI users. I kept a mental tally of them: a well-known economist, a few journalists, and a lot of marketing people. It was like I woke up with a new superpower that could show me who had gotten Botox.
Was I right to feel so judgy? On the one hand, using Claude to write a LinkedIn post probably wasn't that much worse than the crime of posting on LinkedIn at all. Even pre-AI, the site was already overrun with insufferable humblebrags and cringey confessions about a spouse's cancer journey that taught you how to become a better salesperson of cybersecurity software. You don't come to LinkedIn expecting authenticity. Maybe the discovery that, in more than 40% of all longform posts that Pangram analyzed on LinkedIn, people were deploying AI to further their personal branding missions wasn't such a huge deal.
But these posts were showing up next to their supposed authors' names and headshots. In one post, Pang, the startup founder, commiserated with the people who had just gotten laid off at his previous employer. ""A layoff is painful, disorienting, and deeply unfair on a human level,"" the post read. ""If you're affected, please know this is not a reflection of your value or what you're capable of building next."" It's a sweet sentiment that I'm sure his former colleagues appreciated. But according to Pangram, he wasn't the one who wrote those words; Pangram flagged it as 100% AI. So how much of the post did he actually mean, and how much of it was just a bunch of empty words? (""I stand by what it says,"" he told me. ""The reflections in it are mine."")
Next, I turned my spotlight to my work email. Because I'm a reporter, I get inundated with pitches and press releases from publicists, and for a couple of days, I ran them through Pangram. About half included at least a sizable chunk of what Pangram flagged as AI-generated text — a much higher share than on LinkedIn. This didn't bother me. Maybe it was because, like most reporters, I never click into these emails anyway. Or because I never take press releases at face value. It made sense to me that these publicists weren't wasting their time crafting human sentences for a shot in the dark.
Then I remembered a text I received last December from an emergency-room nurse I was briefly dating. It was a clean, polite text breaking things off, but from someone whose texts charmed me for their hurried, punctuation-optional quality, as if she were texting me and performing CPR at the same time. Upon the third read, her two em dashes suddenly glowed in neon colors. It dawned on me: Did AI write this? That inkling — that this person who had kissed me on our third date didn't even have the decency to sweat through the discomfort of rejecting me — hit me hard. I wasn't even worth that to her? But I had no proof, and the suspicion felt so uncharitable I never mentioned it to anyone. Then I forgot about it.

                                  Every time Aki publishes a story, you'll get an alert straight to your inbox!
                              

                                  Stay connected to Aki and get more of their work as it publishes.
                              


                                  By clicking ""Sign up"", you agree to receive emails from Business Insider. In addition, you accept Insider's
                                  Terms of Service and
                                  Privacy Policy.
                                
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Now I finally had a way to know, and against my better judgment, I pasted the text into Pangram. Its verdict: 100% AI. ""Wow,"" one of my friends remarked. ""How lazy. You dodged a bullet."" My sister, ever the optimist, wondered if it was the opposite — that, in an effort to avoid hurting me, the nurse felt compelled to turn to AI to get the rejection just right.
In one of the most awkward texts I've ever sent, I asked the nurse about it. She said she was anxious about the five different versions of the text she had initially drafted herself, so she consulted her coworkers. ""They were like 'just plug it into AI' so we did and that's what it came up with,"" she said. ""They told me I have a tendency to over explain things to people and I don't owe anyone anything."" She added: ""I think making a million decisions a day and having a tendency to over think contributed to just having it to be 'done with.'""
Her response seemed telling of where our world was headed. It's one thing to use AI to write a mass email for work. But there is nothing more intimate than a romantic relationship, and now here was this way to outsource the ending of that to a machine. In the future, will we give up on the pretense of communicating with each other, deploying our bots on our behalf to relay our love and anger and longings? And if that's the case, what's even the point of being alive? This felt like one more way that technology was widening the chasm between each of us, mediating our relationships via screens and social media apps, and now also by large language models. I would have infinitely preferred a blunt breakup text that was real over a polite one from a bot.
Would I ever find love? My chances weren't looking great. But my pessimism barely registered because I was hopped up on my superpower that sorted my world into two neat piles, human and machine. I couldn't imagine going back to my civilian life. I never wanted to get duped into reading anyone's GPT word salad ever again.
This had been the summer of peak AI anger, and the Claude-weary masses were out for blood. In July, YouTube creator Hank Green sparked an uproar when he used a single phrase in a video — ""I appreciate the pushback,"" which is something ChatGPT says a lot — that his fans picked up on. They demanded to know: Was the most authentic guy on the internet using AI to make his videos? Green denied that the phrase in question was written by ChatGPT, but apologized for what he characterized as an overdependence on LLMs to conduct his research. ""Unbelievably disappointing,"" one person replied to his apology. ""Literally nothing you say can be trusted,"" another person wrote.
Seemingly every day, the mob descended on a new target. A lot of those who were outed seemed at least a little chastened. Not the billionaire investor Stanley Druckenmiller, whose op-ed in The Wall Street Journal came back as 100% AI-generated on Pangram. ""Of course I used AI,"" Druckenmiller told The Washington Sun, as if he were a proud coder at a tech company showing off his Claude token usage. ""I write everything using AI now for the same reason I use a calculator when I do math problems."" Critics piled on, prompting Paul Gigot, the Journal's editorial page editor, to defend Druckenmiller. ""The scolding scribes,"" Gigot wrote in a scathing response, didn't understand ""the reality of AI.""
What about my own writing? I use AI, albeit with significant guardrails. As a workplace reporter covering AI, I figured it was important for me to learn what it can and can't do, in part by using it for my own work.
I never use AI to write my first drafts, because the process of writing helps me discover what I actually think. But I've found ChatGPT to be a pretty useful sounding board once I've written a full draft. I'll paste the whole thing in, and ask it to give me broad feedback. Are there sections that are dragging? Are my arguments clear? And from there, I'll zoom in, rewriting the parts it suggests need work. When ChatGPT is unsatisfied with my fix, it'll propose its own fix. Often, I won't like what it suggests, but the back and forth will help me figure out what I'm really trying to say.
I had Pangram ingest my LinkedIn posts from the last four years. I prayed that nothing would get flagged.
Eighty-nine of them came back clean, but a single post from a year ago was marked ""Mixed."" Pangram's analysis said it was 48% AI. I went through my ChatGPT history, and ChatGPT had taken four sentences I had initially drafted and condensed them. It looked like I had taken those edits more or less verbatim, which I told myself I would never do, but apparently I had done it anyway.
The real test, though, was my actual articles. My published stories all came back as 100% human — except for one story it deemed 98% human because of one sentence it said was AI-generated. It turned out that ChatGPT had suggested rewriting my human-written sentence with slightly different phrasing, parts of which I took.
This probably wasn't a crime against humanity. It was 2% of my story, and even within that 2%, the underlying ideas were all mine.
But this whole time, I had been telling myself that I was using AI only as a sounding board. Was that actually true? Even with the machine's help, I wanted everything I put out into the world to be fully me.
Over the last few years, researchers have conducted all kinds of studies to explore our feelings about AI-generated writing. And their findings have been remarkably consistent. In most cases, people actually prefer AI-generated passages over human-written ones, as long as they don't know which is which. But when they're told that what they're reading was generated by AI (or even assisted by AI), their opinion of the passage sours. They also judge the person who used AI as untrustworthy, incompetent, and lazy. Scholars have come to call this the AI disclosure penalty, and they've found it in pretty much every form of writing you can imagine: poetry, work emails, short stories, performance reviews, social media posts, cover letters, dating advice — and news articles.
When I first came across these studies, I assumed that this was one of our many biases as irrational humans — akin to a teacher changing the grade of a student's paper after discovering who wrote it. Regardless of the passage's provenance, it was the same piece of writing! Were we just prejudiced against the machine? But the more I used Pangram, the more reasonable our distaste for AI slop started to seem. To understand why, I turned to Lindsay Brainard, a professor of philosophy at the University of Alabama at Birmingham, who's been writing extensively about what we risk losing when we cede our creativity to AI.
Most conversations about creativity and AI focus on the content of the creative output — in the case of writing, on the words themselves. Brainard argues that we should consider the purpose of the creative output, too. Whether it's an email from a boss, a text from a date, a social media post from a former colleague, or a 3,000-word essay by a stranger, why do we bother reading other people's writing in the first place? Part of it is to obtain information we need, or to be entertained. Brainard argues that we also engage with writing to feel connected to another person — to understand the writer better, based on what they reveal to us in their writing. That's why discovering that something was written by AI feels so alienating. We don't know if the revelations we're grabbing onto are coming from a person with a soul and feelings and life experiences, or from a bot that has none of that. We feel duped. Even with the same assembly of words, the meaning we derive from those words changes depending on who — or what — wrote them. We aren't built to separate the art from the artist.
Of course, not every written communication needs to be so emotionally connected. Sometimes we're just trying to nail down the timing of a meeting, or announce a new peer-to-peer blockchain platform that will revolutionize the tapestry of finance itself. In those cases, the benefits of getting it done quickly with Claude could outweigh whatever relationship-deepening might happen without it. What's important is to weigh that tradeoff — to ask why we're writing what we're writing, and to consider what we might lose when we turn to AI for that writing.
It feels like only a matter of time before our handcrafted sentences get subsumed by the ones LLMs are pumping out at the speed of light. Some 35% of web pages created since the release of ChatGPT show signs of AI authorship, according to the Pew Research Center — up from 18% at the end of 2023. Brainard worries that people will eventually assume everything is AI-generated anyway, and therefore no longer bother to seek evidence of a human mind or soul behind anything they read. In that scenario, people will no longer try to write things themselves — what's the point if people won't read it? — which means they'll reveal less of themselves to other people.
In a world like that, the risk is that we'll feel more and more separate from everyone else. And we'll also lose faith in our own ability to do something that, when you think of it, is kind of miraculous: this ability to communicate the thoughts and feelings that are trapped inside our skulls to another person. For millennia, we've used language to bridge that divide from mind to mind, and we didn't need a statistical algorithm powered by a global fleet of data centers to help us do that. The less we communicate sans AI, the less we'll flex that muscle we're wired to flex — until we lose it, or we never develop it in the first place. ""I tell my students it's really worth it for them to put their own thinking and expression and effort into what they're doing,"" Brainard says. ""It makes them into someone who is worth knowing and someone who other people have a shot at knowing.""
The glimmer of hope, though, is that our collective concern about LLM-generated content is reaching the companies that are our gateways to the web. That's why so many of them have now made managing AI slop a priority. The survival of these platforms might depend on it. Max Spero, CEO and cofounder of Pangram, envisions a future where, even if the majority of the web becomes AI-generated, most of what we actually read on a day-to-day basis will remain what we want it to be: human-written. Just as Google figured out a way to de-rank SEO spam, his hope is that internet companies will figure out a way to de-rank AI slop in our lives. ""As AI gets more and more capable,"" Spero tells me, ""it's clear to me that the human touch is going to be one of the last scarce resources in the world.""
Spero has a zero AI policy when it comes to his own writing, even though he's relinquished his coding to AI. Brainard has a zero AI policy for her writing, too. At the end of our 90-minute call, I asked Brainard something I was nervous to ask: Should I stop using AI to edit my writing? She hesitated, insisting it wasn't her place to tell me what to do. But when I pushed her, she relented. ""You seem so smart and thoughtful and interesting,"" she told me. ""You don't need the AI.""
I wonder if there's still a way to get the best of both worlds. Maybe I can get ChatGPT to act more like a copy editor, flagging just my typos and grammatical errors. Maybe I can get it to act more like a coach, offering me feedback (e.g. ""this section is too long,"") but never rewrite my words. Then again, I also have a great human editor who already does that and so much more. He pushes me to say the bigger and deeper and sharper thing without ever doing it for me. He takes pains to preserve my quirks in my writing — my warmth, my humor, my earnestness, my self-doubt.
I should trust him to do that for me, and I should trust myself to say what I mean to say, and I should trust you — that what you want is the real me and not a machine-polished version of me.

96,2026-09-28,"Warren Buffett's retirement hasn't distracted Berkshire Hathaway from doing what he did best — snapping up beaten-down stocks.
The legendary investor's conglomerate has nearly doubled its stake in homebuilder Lennar since the end of June, regulatory filings show.
Buffett, 96, departed as CEO at the turn of this year and stepped down as chairman on September 18.
Three days later, Berkshire revealed it owned more than 10% of Lennar, after boosting its total amount of Class A and Class B shares from about 13.4 million at the end of June to 21.5 million on September 17.
Berkshire disclosed in subsequent filings that as of Friday, it holds about 25.4 million shares, for a nearly 11% stake valued at around $2.1 billion. It spent about $349 million on Lennar stock between September 17 and 25, the disclosures show.
The conglomerate — which owns businesses such as Geico and Fruit of the Loom and holds large stakes in public companies such as Apple and American Express — established its stake in Lennar in the first half of last year.
The bet fits smoothly within Buffett's signature value-investing framework. Lennar shares have slumped 21% this year to about $82, less than half their peak price two years ago.
The nation's second-largest homebuilder has been hit by a cooling US housing market. The war in Iran has rekindled inflation fears and ratcheted up borrowing costs, exacerbating an affordability crisis for prospective homebuyers.
Buffett became one of history's most successful investors by buying stocks and businesses at bargain prices then holding them for the long term, garnering him enormous returns on his money.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

While running Berkshire from 1965 through 2025, the company's stock price rose by 6,100,000%, trouncing the benchmark S&P 500's 46,000% total return over the same period.
It's worth noting that Lennar is a small position within Berkshire's roughly $300 billion stock portfolio. The size of the bet suggests that Berkshire's investment manager, Ted Weschler, is behind it rather than Buffett himself, likely with the approval of new CEO Greg Abel.

98,2026-09-28,"As humanity continues to move towards a permanent settlement on the Moon, we still have at least one big hurdle to face - the lunar night, which lasts for roughly fourteen days straight and can drop temperatures to a bone-chilling -223Â°C. In order to survive those conditions, we need a power source that doesnât come and go with the Sun, or rely on non-existent stock of coal, gas or wind. In other words, we need nuclear power, and a new paper, available in pre-print on arXiv by Julius Mercz of the Technical University of Munich and his co-authors, describes a new type of nuclear reactor that wouldnât just power a habitatâs environmental systems, but also enable its materials extraction technologies as well.
âLiving off the landâ on the Moon (or in-situ resource utilization - ISRU - as space exploration fans like to call it) is going to be critical to any long-term lunar base. Shipping all the necessary supplies to build and maintain a lunar base up from Earth is prohibitively expensive, costing tens of thousands of dollars per kilogram. Luckily, much of the material we would need to build those bases are actually available on the Moon itself - theyâre just locked up in the fine dust on the surface that we call regolith.
To extract oxygen and useful industrial materials from regolith, scientists have turned to a process called Molten Salt Electrolysis. This works by dissolving moon dust in liquid salt, running an electrical current through it, and stripping out oxygen, which leaves behind metallic alloys that are useful in building structures. However, it is notoriously energy hungry, requiring temperatures upwards of 900Â°C to work correctly.

Fraser talks about the Kilopower reactor - NASA's answer to supplying power on the Moon.
In traditional lunar base plans, those temperatures are supplied by electric heaters, powered by some sort of electrical system - typically nuclear or solar. In the process of converting energy into electricity, though, approximately 60% of it is lost as waste heat, severely crimping the efficiency of such systems when attempting to power energy-hungry processes.
Enter the Microreactor Utilisation for Lunar Exploration (MULE) reactor - which could be a reference to either the Foundation Series or Starcraft - though it's unclear if either of those sci-fi worlds had any influence on the systemâs name. There are a couple of unique aspects about the MULE reactor, so first letâs concentrate on its thermal chain.
Instead of directly converting the thermal energy generated by the nuclear fission reactions that power it, MULE intentionally implements a âcascadeâ of its thermal energy. The first step in that cascade is powering the MSE reaction that is critical for the long-term survival of any permanent moon base. The temperatures coming out of the plant are expected to be around 1000Â°C, making it in the range necessary to power the MSE reaction. In addition, the system would add a thermal storage bank - similar to a battery that stores heat instead of electricity - to even out the thermal load supplied to the reaction, and maintain it if the MULE is shut down for some reason.

Fraser talks about how to improve nuclear reactors for space exploration.
The next step in the cascade is electrical power generation, which the paper authors expected to take place around 750Â°C. This uses a closed-loop Brayton cycle - basically using the expanding gas to power a turbine, which then generates electricity as per normal. After shedding most of its thermal energy into the turbine, the gas is cooled to a still blistering 150Â°C, but this temperature is intentionally used to heat the habitats themselves, obviously with some thermal controls in place so that the occupants donât get blasted with gas above the boiling temperature of water. Finally, any residual heat from the process is dumped into thermal radiators at 75Â°C and bled into deep space as infrared light.
A second unique feature of the MULE is its actual reactor design. Instead of using standard fuel rods, it uses an all-ceramic core made primarily out of silicon carbide, with 37 hexagonal fuel assemblies loaded with TRIstructural-ISOtropic particles (TRISO). Each of these poppy-seed sized pellets includes a uranium-carbide fuel kernel nestled inside three concentric layers of carbon and ceramic protective shells. Each acts as a miniature pressure vessel, ensuring that, no matter what happens in the rest of the reactor, the fuel is virtually melt-proof.
Thatâs a good thing too, as the fuel itself would be enriched to 93% U-235 - not quite weapons grade, but much higher than the typical 2-3 percent enrichment used in Earth-based reactors. Another unique feature is, instead of control rods, the MULE uses six pivot controlled drums around the coreâs perimeter. One side of this drum is coated in a neutron absorber, such as boron-carbide, whereas the other side, which is coated in beryllium oxide, acts as a neutron reflector. Critically, the entire system measures just 2.3m long by 0.78m wide, and weighs only 2.1 metric tons - well within the payload fairing capabilities of todayâs heavy lift rockets like Starship or New Glenn.

Fraser gives a realistic take on why Moon cities are likely not going to happen anytime soon.
To prove the system worked, the authors turned to simulations, since getting an actual experimental nuclear reactor up and running isâ¦difficult. The software they used is known as Serpent 2 - a type of Monte-Carlo simulator that allowed them to calculate the physics of millions of individual neutrons as they ricocheted their way around the reactor. In the simulation, the authors set a goal of a ten year operational life with the fuel they had.
They absolutely blew that out of the water. After ten years of the simulation, the control drums had only rotated about 5.34 degrees, and upwards of 98% of the original uranium fuel remained unused. After pushing the system to go farther, the researchers found that, in its current configuration, MULE could operate for 95 years without refueling.
Thatâs not to say there wouldnât be problems along the way. One obvious one would be the materials the core is made out of - managing hot helium (the gas used for thermal transfer) is not easy, and ceramics have a habit of breaking if they are thermally shocked too much. Another engineering challenge is shielding. The current design doesnât use any shielding to protect itself from radiation. Instead, the authors suggest burying it under a few meters of lunar regolith itself - perhaps that Starcraftian technology is where the system got its name. But in real life, we donât have any lunar excavating technology that could dig a hole big enough to fit one of these reactors. 
It might not be too long before we develop one, though. Excavation is one critical feature of any long-term lunar base, and powering any such will require the industrial muscle of a nuclear reactor. At that point, weâre at a bit of a chicken-and-egg standpoint. But knowing that engineers have come up with this potential option to power future permanent lunar colonies is certainly a good thing. It just might be awhile before we see such a reactor on (or under) the lunar surface.

UT - The 2030 Race for a Moon Reactor

UT - NASA Funds the Development of a Nuclear Reactor on the Moon That Would Last for 10 Years
Andy has been interested in space exploration ever since reading Pale Blue Dot in middle school. An engineer by training, he likes to focus on the practical challenges of space exploration, whether that's getting rid of perchlorates on Mars or making ultra-smooth mirrors to capture ever clearer data. When not writing or engineering things he can be found entertaining his four children, six cats, and two dogs, or running in circles and throwing plastic discs at people to stay in shape.

99,2026-09-28,"At times we forget what an amazing wonder the Bundesrepublik Deutschland was.  At the end of the World War II, Germany was one of the sickest and cruelest human societies in history, ever.  Not too many years later, it was one of the best and most successful societies ever.
By the 1980s, living standards had caught up to the United States, with the provision of public goods sometimes superior.  The country was fully democratic, pro-Western, and largely pro-American.
Their rail system and postal service were amongst the best ever created.
For thinkers of note there were Hans Blumenberg, Habermas, Peter Weiss, Gadamer, late Heidegger and late Carl Schmitt, Reinhart Koselleck, the underrated Klaus Theweleit, Niklas Luhmann, and perhaps you value some of the other members of the Frankfurt School.
The visual arts were very strong, with Richter, Polke, Baselitz, Beuys, Penck, Palermo, and much more.
Music produced Stockhausen, Henze, Lachenmann, Rihm, Zimmerman, Kraftwerk, Can, and all of Krautrock, later techno, though right at the time of unification rather than during the BRD per se.  The list of vocalists, instrumentalists, and conductors is strong.  Was there anywhere better for hearing opera?
Perhaps I prefer the fiction from Austria and Switzerland, but at the very least Germany provided a major market for those authors and it was an extraordinarily literate country with amazing bookstores.  For domestic authors there were Böll, Patrick Süskind, Siegfried Lenz, Wolfgang Koeppen, can I count Uwe Johnson?, and Arno Schmidt maybe?  I do not like Grass, but it seems wrong not to list him.
The food could be very good, especially in the southwest.  There were Michelin star restaurants all over the country (still are, to be clear on this point).  So many well-functioning cities, with many of the world’s best transit systems.  Lots of nuclear power and a strong industrial base.  West Berlin was an exciting city with an air of mystery.  Some might say no speed limit on the Autobahn, though I am less sure that was a virtue.  Plenty of beautiful women and reasonable attitudes toward sex.
If you had to choose, what was the worst thing about the country?  No shopping on Sundays?  Workplace and shopping hours discrimination against women?  Too much smoking?  Obsession with Waldsterben?
Are there features of post-unification Germany that can compare to this earlier era?  So much seems not to work well.  So many policy mistakes have been made.  So much leadership lost in the areas mentioned above.  So much pessimism, sadly a lot of it seems to be justified.
Where did all the good performance go?  And why did it leave?  Lack of a communist enemy?  Absorption of East Germany?  The simple accretion of distance from pre-WWII German creativity?
The wonder that was the Bundesrepublik Deutschland.  Johannes, we hardly knew ye.
